A First Proof Sprint
J. Corneli

Abstract




Reproducibility: built August 10, 2026 from

futon6
version-controlled git history (c92a4eb).

Source:
https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6

Abstract

This monograph reports a multi-agent proof sprint on ten research-level
problems, combining rapid draft generation with adversarial verification,
targeted repair, and explicit provenance. The workflow uses wiring-diagram
decompositions of claim dependencies to localize gaps and coordinate
reviewer-driven revisions. Final outcomes are heterogeneous but explicit:
the manuscript distinguishes
mathematical status
from

QC-validation status
. In the current ledger, Problems 1, 2, 3, and 8
are treated as mathematically closed with validation-complete records
(Problem 2 still depends on the cited newvector/test-vector theorem input);
Problems 5 and 7 are mathematically closed with partial QC status (scope-limited
closure for Problem 5, and an independent theorem-ledger re-check pending for
Problem 7). Problem 4 is partial: proved analytically for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}3
, and
proved for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4
via the Path 2 algebraic certificate, with higher
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n

open. Problem 6 is partial: the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
case is proved with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}1/3
, while
the general-graph bridge remains open (formal
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bar{d}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}1
bound at

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}\neq 0
). Problem 9 has strong computational evidence but lacks a clean
algebraic closure of the universal converse step. Problem 10 remains
conditional under explicitly stated assumptions. The main methodological result
is that structure-aware verification and layer-switching strategies improve
reliability and calibration in compressed proof sprints.

Highlights

•

Dependency-graph representations of proofs enabled local gap isolation and targeted repair across all ten problems.

•

Node-level verification with predecessor/successor context improved calibration relative to whole-draft review.

•

Adversarial critic–responder iteration converted early overclaims into explicit closed, partial, or conditional outcomes.

•

Targeted computational stress tests constrained speculative branches, especially in the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4
branch of Problem 4.

•

Structured handoff memos preserved unresolved obligations as named, testable next steps rather than implicit deferrals.

Snapshot

•

Ten initial proof attempts drafted in under two hours.

•

Over two hundred commits across roughly 55 hours wall-clock (February 11–13, 2026), in addition to earlier infrastructure development.

•

A shift in framing over the course of the sprint: from an early “all solved” posture to a clearer partition into proved, conditional, and partial results.

•

A methodological lesson: wiring-diagram–based verification localized gaps and inconsistencies more effectively than plain narrative drafting.

Introduction

This monograph records both the mathematical results and the working process
behind a compressed, multi-agent proof cycle in
futon6
.
1
1

1

Source repository:
https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6
The project
began as infrastructure—knowledge ingestion, tagging, and wiring-diagram
metatheory—and then pivoted into a rapid proof sprint marked by iterative
critique and repair.

The working process came to resemble Lakatos’s
Proofs and
Refutations

[
1
]
more closely than anticipated. In Lakatos’s
dialogue, a conjecture is proposed, counterexamples emerge, the conjecture is
revised, and the cycle repeats. Each iteration sharpens both the statement and
the participants’ understanding. Our sprint followed a similar rhythm, except
that the interlocutors were a human dispatcher, multiple Claude instances
(provers), and Codex (critic and research assistant). Conjectures were drafted
rapidly; adversarial review exposed overclaims and hidden gaps; targeted repair
narrowed the remaining obligations.

The wiring-diagram decomposition—representing each proof as a typed graph of
claims and dependencies—served as the shared epistemic artefact. Where
Lakatos’s fictional classroom had a blackboard, this project had a structured
proof graph. That graph made disagreements local and repairable.

The title alludes to the sprint format: ten open problems attempted in
roughly 55 hours wall-clock, with both process and outcomes documented.
The Lakatosian connection also runs through Pease et
al.
[
2
]
, which formalised Lakatos’s informal logic of
mathematical discovery as a dialogue game over argumentation structures,
implemented in a system capable of mixed-initiative human–AI collaboration.
That paper asked whether computers could participate in mathematical reasoning
through conjecture, critique, and reform. The present work can be read as an
empirical extension of that question: ten research problems attempted by AI
agents under human supervision, with the Lakatosian cycle unfolding at machine
speed.

In a related strand of work, Corneli et al.
[
3
]
,
developed a computational model of mathematical question-and-answer dialogues
using Inference Anchoring Theory + Content (IATC) to annotate MathOverflow
threads with performative types
(assert, challenge, reform, clarify, etc.). The same vocabulary reappears in
this project’s Stage 7 thread-wiring pipeline (see Part III, Act VI), where
StackExchange threads are parsed into wiring diagrams whose edges carry the
same performative labels. Earlier work on peer-produced mathematical
knowledge
[
5
]
established the empirical base: PlanetMath as a
community-maintained proof ecosystem, studied through the lens of paragogy
(peer-produced peer learning). The present sprint can be read as a
machine-accelerated instance of the same phenomenon: collaborative,
incremental proof construction with explicit argumentation structure.

The working notes reveal a persistent asymmetry: generation is fast;
trustworthy validation is slow. Lakatos identified the same imbalance in his
classroom: conjectures are cheap, but understanding
why
they fail (or
hold) is the real work. In this sprint, the strongest leverage came not from
first-pass drafting quality, but from structured verification—tools that made
weaknesses visible early, locally, and precisely.

Materials and Methods

•

Model/runtime split.
Claude Max runs on a remote Linode host; Codex Pro runs on the local laptop workspace (
/home/joe/code/futon6
).

•

Software/toolchain summary.
Computational checks used Python 3
scripts with NumPy/SciPy for numerical linear algebra and optimization, SymPy
for symbolic algebra (resultants, Sturm sequences, Gröbner bases), mpmath
for selected high-precision checks, PHCpack via
phcpy
for
polynomial-system solving/certification in Problem 4 (Case 3c), and cvxpy for
SDP-based sum-of-squares certificate attempts. Proof-structure analysis used
wiring-diagram generators (nine scripts producing JSON and Mermaid flowcharts
via the internal
thread_performatives
module) and
validate-ct.py

for classical NER and scope extraction over PlanetMath entries. Pattern
tagging and term spotting used Babashka (Clojure) scripts operating on EDN
data formats. Document production used
L
a
T
e
X
(
pdflatex
) with
repository consistency checks via
check-latex-text-truth.py
,

check-latex-syntax-violations.py
, and

check-ratchet-fixedw-typesetting.py
.

Reader Guide

This document is organized in five parts.

•

Part I (Annotated Overview):
short, citation-rich introductions for Problems 1–10, intended as navigational summaries.

•

Part II (Full Proof Drafts):
expanded writeups imported from the solution files, suitable as the base for a longer manuscript (targeting roughly book-length exposition). Coloured boxes separate the proof proper from process annotations (dead paths, revisions, open obligations).

•

Part III (Prompt Excerpts and Pivot Moments):
curated source excerpts (prompts and dispatch/history snippets) that document key decision points and proof-state transitions.

•

Part IV (Proof Patterns):
a cross-problem analysis of strategy patterns—what worked (layer-switching, creative reduction, structural decomposition), what didn’t (the TryHarder loop), and how a coaching intervention broke a stuck problem open.

•

Part V (Color Plates):
syntax-coloured renderings of the proof files, produced by automated math-mode normalization.

Terminology

AIF

Active Inference Framework—used here as a conceptual lens for agent roles, intervention points, and coordination state; a full AIF policy loop for proof search is proposed future work, not yet implemented in this sprint.

BSS

Batson–Spielman–Srivastava spectral sparsification framework.

IATC

Inference Anchoring Theory + Content—an annotation scheme for mathematical dialogues used here to type dialogue moves and content links within the AIF process model
[
3
]
.

MSS

Marcus–Spielman–Srivastava interlacing families framework.

PSD

Positive semi-definite (for matrices).

Part I
Annotated Overview

Chapter 1
Problem 1: Measure Equivalence Under Smooth Shifts of the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{4}_{3}
Measure

Problem statement

Given the renormalized
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{4}_{3}
measure
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{T}^{3}
, and a smooth nonzero shift
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
, determine whether
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
and the shifted measure
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{*}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
are equivalent.

Answer

Yes:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}\sim T_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{*}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
.

Status Note

Mathematical claim treated as closed, with unified repair checks
and the canonical node-level verifier record aligned on the key equivalence
chain repairs.

Annotated proof sketch

1.

Use absolute continuity of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
with respect to the Gaussian reference measure
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{0}
from variational construction results for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{4}_{3}
1
1

1

Barashkov–Gubinelli, arXiv:
https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.10529
.
.

2.

Apply Cameron--Martin to the Gaussian part: smooth shifts lie in the relevant Cameron--Martin space and preserve equivalence
2
2

2

Background discussion:
https://mathoverflow.net/questions/194307/cameron-martin-theorem-non-gaussian-shift
.
.

3.

Control the interaction change
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})-V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})
using Wick expansion and exponential integrability for the cubic perturbation. This is the renormalized step tied to regularity structures / paracontrolled technology
3
3

3

Hairer, arXiv:
https://arxiv.org/abs/1303.5113
; Gubinelli–Imkeller–Perkowski, arXiv:
https://arxiv.org/abs/1210.2684
.
.

The Radon–Nikodym derivative is strictly positive and integrable in both directions, so mutual absolute continuity follows.

Selected citations

•

Variational
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{4}_{3}
:
https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.10529

•

Regularity structures:
https://arxiv.org/abs/1303.5113

Paracontrolled distributions:
https://arxiv.org/abs/1210.2684

•

Cameron–Martin discussion:
https://mathoverflow.net/questions/194307/cameron-martin-theorem-non-gaussian-shift

Background and prerequisites

Field:
Stochastic PDE / Measure Theory.

What you need:
Measure theory and Gaussian measures (Cameron–Martin theorem), plus familiarity with either regularity structures or paracontrolled distributions for making sense of the renormalized
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{4}_{3}
interaction.

Way in:
Hairer, “An Introduction to Stochastic PDEs” (lecture notes, arXiv:0907.4178) for the probabilistic side; the Barashkov–Gubinelli variational paper cited below is largely self-contained for the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{4}_{3}
-specific machinery.

Chapter 2
Problem 2: Universal Test Vector for Rankin–Selberg Integrals

Problem statement

For generic irreducible admissible representations
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}1}(F)
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
over a non-archimedean local field, ask whether one can fix a single Whittaker vector
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W
in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
such that some
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V
in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
makes the twisted local Rankin–Selberg integral finite and nonzero for all complex
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}s
.

Answer

Yes: the essential/new Whittaker vector
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{0}
serves as a universal test vector.

Status Note

Core local algebraic steps are treated as repaired; the result still
depends on the cited newvector/test-vector theorem input

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{FW}})
. Canonical node-level verifier outputs are now
aligned with the repaired lemma chain.

Annotated proof sketch

1.

The set of local zeta integrals spans a fractional ideal in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{\pm s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
, which is a PID
1
1

1

PID refresher:
https://planetmath.org/principalidealdomain
.
. Hence a monomial reduction criterion is available.

2.

Evaluate the twisted Kirillov function
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}(g)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W_{0}(\mathrm{diag}(g,1)u_{Q})
at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I_{n}
to prove nontriviality; this uses the Whittaker transformation law.

3.

Use
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-equivariance and irreducibility of the Kirillov model restriction to show that fixing any nonzero
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
(hence
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}
) still generates the full local
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L
-factor ideal.

This yields existence of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V
with integral equal to a nonzero monomial in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q_{F}^{-s}
, so it is finite and nonvanishing for all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}s
.

Selected citations

•

Jacquet–Piatetski-Shapiro–Shalika (Rankin–Selberg local theory):
https://www.jstor.org/stable/2374430

•

Bernstein–Zelevinsky (representations of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}(n,F)
):
http://mi.mathnet.ru/eng/rm4200

MathOverflow reference thread on local test vectors:
https://mathoverflow.net/questions/469865/reference-request-test-vectors-for-local-rankin-selberg-l-factors-in-ramified-c

•

Rankin–Selberg overview thread:
https://mathoverflow.net/questions/223233/jacquets-approach-to-rankin-selberg-l-functions

Background and prerequisites

Field:
Automorphic Forms / Representation Theory of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p
-adic Groups.

What you need:
Smooth representations of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
over non-archimedean local fields, Whittaker models, and the Rankin–Selberg integral formalism for local
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L
-factors. Graduate-level algebraic number theory assumed.

Way in:
Bump,
Automorphic Forms and Representations
(Cambridge, 1997), especially Part II on local theory; Cogdell’s expository article “
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L
-functions and Converse Theorems for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
” (Fields Institute lectures).

Chapter 3
Problem 3: Markov Chain with ASEP Polynomial Stationary Distribution

Problem statement

Construct a nontrivial Markov chain on permutations of a partition
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
whose stationary distribution is the normalized interpolation ASEP polynomial weight at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}1
.

Answer

Yes: the inhomogeneous multispecies
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t
-PushTASEP yields the required stationary law.

Annotated proof sketch

1.

Define a finite-state continuous-time Markov chain with explicit local transition rates that depend only on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(x,t)
and species order (not on polynomial values).

2.

Invoke the stationary measure theorem identifying the stationary weights with ASEP/Macdonald objects at

Ayyer–Martin–Williams, arXiv:
https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.10485
.
.

Chapter 4
Problem 4: Root Separation Energy Under Finite Free Convolution (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4
)

Problem statement

For monic real-rooted degree-
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n
polynomials, test

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p\boxplus_{n}q)}\geq\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p)}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(q)}.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}3

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4

P-convexity of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{\mathrm{red}}
); higher
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n

Status Note

Problem 4 is proved for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}3

and proved for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4

identity

Chapter 5
Problem 5:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{O}
-Slice Connectivity via Geometric Fixed Points

Problem statement

For an incomplete transfer system from an
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{\infty}
operad, define the associated restricted regular slice filtration and characterize
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{O}
-slice connectivity using geometric fixed points.

Answer

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}
-local connective
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}G
-spectra, one can restrict the Hill–Yarnall connectivity criterion to subgroups in the admissible family
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Solved in a scope-limited form: the subgroup-family-level characterization is established for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}
-local connective spectra; a full indexing-system-level extension is left open.

Status Note

Closed only in the stated local scope

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}\text{-local connective spectra})
. The
indexing-system-level extension remains an explicit open obligation.

Open Gap / Conditional Step.
Open obligation (scope extension): move from subgroup-family indexing
to full Blumberg–Hill indexing-system data (admissible finite

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H
-sets, not only admissible subgroups). The current result uses

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}H:e{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}H\ \text{admissible}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
and does not yet
encode finer
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}H
admissibility for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}e
. Closing this gap
requires a full indexing-system formulation of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{O}
-slice
connectivity and, in parallel, a non-local replacement (or discharge) of the

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}
-locality hypothesis in the reverse direction.

Annotated proof sketch

1.

Build
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}^{\mathcal{O}}_{\geq n}
from regular slice cells indexed only by admissible subgroups.

2.

Use closure properties of indexing systems to verify that this restriction is stable under conjugation and subgroup passage.

3.

Adapt Hill–Yarnall’s geometric fixed-point criterion: for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}
-local spectra, no tests are needed outside
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}
because those geometric fixed points vanish by locality.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{\infty}

https://arxiv.org/abs/1309.1750

What you need:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}G
-spectra, geometric fixed-point functors, the slice filtration of Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{\infty}

Chapter 6
Problem 6:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}
-Light Vertex Subsets of Graphs

Problem statement

Find a universal constant
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}0
such that every weighted graph and every
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}(0,1)
admit a subset
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S
with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\geq c_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}n,\qquad L_{G\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}S\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}L_{G}.

Partial (general case conditional). The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
case is proved with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}1/3
;
for general graphs, the remaining issue is the Barrier Maintenance Invariant
(BMI): prove the full barrier-degree bound
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bar{d}_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}1
when
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}0
.
The current E/F regime reduction isolates this to two explicit lemmas.

Status Note

Unconditional closure holds for the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
branch. The general-graph
theorem remains conditional on two explicit BMI regime lemmas in the

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}\neq 0
branch.

Annotated proof structure

1.

Use leverage scores
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}w_{e}R_{\mathrm{eff}}(u,v)
to define the heavy-edge graph and show any
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}
-light set must be independent there.

2.

Apply the leverage-score trace identity and Turan’s theorem to extract a large candidate set.

3.

Resolve easy branches (independent-in-
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}G
and directly bounded spectral sum) unconditionally.

4.

Reduce the hard branch to barrier maintenance (BMI), then split by a
graph-adaptive E/F regime decomposition; substantial computational evidence is
available, but the final general proof still requires two regime lemmas.

Open Gap / Conditional Step.
The unresolved step is the full BMI bound in the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}0
regime.
The writeup reduces this to two explicit E/F regime lemmas. Until those lemmas
are proved (or replaced by an equivalent closure argument), the
universal-constant theorem remains conditional.

Chapter 7
Problem 7: Uniform Lattice with 2-Torsion and Rationally Acyclic Universal Cover

Problem statement

Can a cocompact lattice
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
in a real semisimple Lie group, containing an element of order
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}2
, be the fundamental group of a closed manifold whose universal cover is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{Q}
-acyclic?

Provisionally closed via the rotation route (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}7
, congruence setting):
E2 is discharged and the S-branch is supported by the codim-2 surgery theorem
chain documented in the project theorem-number ledger,

https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem7-g2-theorem-chain.md
with final
confidence pending independent line-by-line ledger re-check.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{FH}(\mathbb{Q})

Chapter 8
Problem 8: Lagrangian Smoothing of 4-Valent Polyhedral Surfaces

Problem statement

If every vertex of a polyhedral Lagrangian surface in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(\mathbb{R}^{4},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega})
is 4-valent, must the surface admit a Lagrangian smoothing through Hamiltonian isotopy?

Answer

Yes.

Status Note

Current draft treats the geometric argument as closed, with unified
repair checks and the canonical node-level verifier record aligned on the
main algebraic and Maslov-index repairs.

Annotated proof sketch

1.

Analyze a 4-valent vertex: edge vectors span
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{4}
, forcing a symplectic splitting into two 2D factors.

2.

Write the local model as a product of two planar corners and smooth each corner separately.

3.

Check Lagrangian condition factorwise (product of isotropic curves in 2D symplectic factors).

4.

Smooth remaining edge creases via exact-graph interpolation and glue local constructions by disjoint supports.

5.

Promote to Hamiltonian isotopy using exactness/flux-vanishing in local models and composition closure of compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopies.

Selected citations

•

Weinstein neighborhood theorem source paper:
https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/000187087190005X

•

McDuff–Salamon textbook page:
https://global.oup.com/academic/product/introduction-to-symplectic-topology-9780198794899

•

Symplectic manifold definition:
https://planetmath.org/symplecticmanifold

•

Lagrangian submanifold definition:
https://planetmath.org/lagrangiansubmanifold

Background and prerequisites

Field:
Symplectic Geometry / Lagrangian Topology.

What you need:
Symplectic manifolds, Lagrangian submanifolds, Hamiltonian isotopy, and Weinstein neighborhood theorems. The proof uses only local symplectic techniques (no Floer theory).

Way in:
McDuff–Salamon,
Introduction to Symplectic Topology
(OUP, 3rd ed., 2017)—the standard graduate textbook covering all prerequisites.

Chapter 9
Problem 9: Polynomial Detection of Rank-1 Scaling for Quadrifocal Tensors

Problem statement

Given generic camera matrices and quadrifocal tensors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Q^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}
, construct a polynomial map
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F
of degree bounded independently of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n
that vanishes exactly on rank-1 separable scaling tensors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}v_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}w_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}x_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
.

Answer

Yes: one can use degree-3 determinant constraints (all relevant
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}3{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}3
minors).

Status Note

Forward and witness checks are strong, but full closure still hinges
on a formal universal converse argument (all non-rank-1
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
, not only
tested families). Treat as partial until that algebraic universality step is
written cleanly into the proof chain.

Annotated proof sketch

1.

For fixed
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},k,\ell)
, the determinant pairing defines an alternating bilinear form of rank
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}2
on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{4}
, so all induced
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}3{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}3
minors vanish.

2.

If
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
is rank-1 separable, Hadamard-diagonal scaling preserves the rank-
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}2
property, hence all these minors vanish.

3.

Conversely, vanishing of all minors across the three matricization patterns forces each matricization rank to be
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}1
, and compatibility implies full tensor separability.

4.

Collect all such minors into
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F
; each coordinate has degree
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}3
and no dependence on camera parameters.

Selected citations

•

Segre embedding (rank-1 tensor variety):
https://planetmath.org/segreembedding

•

Determinantal varieties:
https://planetmath.org/determinantalvariety

•

Tensor rank definition:
https://planetmath.org/tensorrank

•

Multiple-view geometry reference text:
https://www.robots.ox.ac.uk/˜vgg/hzbook/

Background and prerequisites

Field:
Algebraic Geometry / Computer Vision (Multiview Geometry).

What you need:
Tensor algebra (rank, matricizations, Segre varieties), determinantal varieties, and the basics of multiview geometry (camera matrices, multi-focal tensors). The algebraic geometry is classical; no scheme theory required.

Way in:
Hartley–Zisserman,
Multiple View Geometry in Computer Vision
(Cambridge, 2nd ed.) for the applied setting; Landsberg,
Tensors: Geometry and Applications
(AMS, 2012) for the algebraic underpinnings.

Chapter 10
Problem 10: PCG for RKHS-Constrained Tensor CP Decomposition

Problem statement

Explain why preconditioned conjugate gradient (PCG) solves the mode-
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}k
RKHS CP subproblem without
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(N)
per-iteration cost, where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N
is the full ambient sample size.

Answer

Use implicit Kronecker/Khatri–Rao matrix-vector products and a Kronecker preconditioner built from expected sampling geometry.

Status Note

This result is
conditional
: it is closed under explicit
assumptions (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}0
,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}\succ 0
, and sampling/coherence conditions
controlling preconditioned conditioning). Necessity counterexamples are
recorded in the full writeup when these assumptions are dropped.

Annotated proof sketch

1.

Never form the full system matrix; compute
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Ax
through structured operations:
kernel multiplies, sparse sampling/restriction, and Khatri–Rao contractions.

2.

Build
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}b
by the same structure, again avoiding dense
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N
-scale tensors.

3.

Precondition with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}K_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}
from replacing sparse sampling by its expectation; invert
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P
via two small Cholesky solves.

4.

Apply standard PCG convergence bounds in terms of condition number
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}(P^{-1/2}AP^{-1/2})
and matrix concentration for random sampling.

Selected citations

•

Matrix concentration / Freedman-style bound context:
https://arxiv.org/abs/1110.1379

•

FALKON large-scale kernel method:
https://arxiv.org/abs/1705.10958

•

Kronecker product definition:
https://planetmath.org/kroneckerproduct

•

Conjugate gradient method definition:
https://planetmath.org/conjugategradientmethod

Background and prerequisites

Field:
Numerical Linear Algebra / Kernel Methods / Tensor Decomposition.

What you need:
Conjugate gradient and preconditioning, Kronecker and Khatri–Rao products, reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS), and matrix concentration inequalities for random sampling operators.

Way in:
Trefethen–Bau,
Numerical Linear Algebra
(SIAM, 1997) for iterative methods; Rasmussen–Williams,
Gaussian Processes for Machine Learning
(MIT Press, 2006; freely available online) for RKHS context.

Reading Convention

This manuscript uses boxed annotations selectively. In the current revision,
the full proof chapters are primarily unboxed; process boxes appear only where
there is substantive process context to preserve.

•

Unboxed text
is the proof proper. A reader following the
five-page-per-problem brief can read only the unboxed text and obtain a
self-contained argument (or, where an argument is incomplete, a clearly
stated conditional result).

•

Status Note boxes
appear in the annotated overview chapters to
summarize closure/QC state for each problem.

•

Process Note boxes
appear in process-facing parts (for example,
prompt excerpts) when methodological scope or reviewer context is needed.

•

Dead-path / revision / open-obligation semantics are maintained in the
tooling vocabulary, but are not instantiated as standalone boxed blocks in
the current full-proof chapter text.

Chapter 11
Problem 1: Equivalence of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
Measure Under Smooth Shifts

11.1
Problem Statement

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
be the 3D unit torus. Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
be the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
measure on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}D^{\prime}(T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}R
be a smooth nonzero function and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}(u)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
the
shift map. Are
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
equivalent (same null sets)?

11.2
Answer

Yes.
The measures
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
are equivalent.

Confidence: Medium-high.
The argument combines Cameron-Martin theory with
the absolute continuity structure of the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
construction (Barashkov-Gubinelli
2020, Theorem 1.1). The integrability bound is stated for a neighborhood of the
required exponent, not for all t, matching the available log-Sobolev technology.

11.3
Solution

11.3.1
1. The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
measure

The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
measure
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}D^{\prime}(T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
is the probability measure formally
written as:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}d{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Z^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(-V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}))d{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
is the Gaussian free field (GFF) measure with covariance
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}m^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Delta}
)ˆ
{
-1
}
on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
, and the interaction is:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}_{T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}(:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}:{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}C:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}:)\mathrm{d}x

Here :
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{k}
: denotes the k-th Wick power (renormalized product), and C is a
mass counterterm that diverges under regularization removal.

The rigorous construction (Hairer 2014, Gubinelli-Imkeller-Perkowski 2015,
Barashkov-Gubinelli 2020) produces
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
as a well-defined probability measure
on distributions of regularity
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}}(T^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}).

11.3.2
2. Absolute continuity with respect to the GFF

Key fact:
The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
measure
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
is equivalent to (has the same null
sets as) the base Gaussian free field measure
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}~{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

This equivalence follows from the variational construction of
Barashkov-Gubinelli (2020, Theorem 1.1), which establishes

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}E_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(-V)\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}\infty
and hence
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}\ll{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
with strictly positive
density exp(-V(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
))/Z. Since exp(-V(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}))\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
a.s. (exponential is always
positive), the reverse absolute continuity
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\ll{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
also holds.

11.3.3
3. Cameron-Martin theory for the GFF

For the Gaussian free field
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
with covariance C
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(m^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Delta})^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},

the Cameron-Martin space is:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})(\text{Sobolev space of order}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})

Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
is smooth,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}C^{\infty}(T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
subset
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
, so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
is in the
Cameron-Martin space.

Cameron-Martin theorem:
For any
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}h{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}H
, the shifted Gaussian measure

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{h}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}
is equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, with Radon-Nikodym derivative:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{d}T_{h}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}/d{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(l_{h}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}\|h\|_{H^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}l_{h}
is the linear functional associated to h. In particular:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}~{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

11.3.4
4. Shift of the interacting measure

To compute
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu},
we need the density of the shifted interacting measure:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{d}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}/d{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Z^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(-V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(l_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}\|{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}\|_{H^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

The shifted interaction V(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
) expands (using Wick ordering relative to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
):

\displaystyle V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}:\mathrm{d}x{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}:\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}:\mathrm{d}x{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-C({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}:\mathrm{d}x{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}:\mathrm{d}x{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrm{d}x){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}(\text{renorm}.\text{corrections})

Renormalization under shift:
The term 6 int
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
: dx generates an
additional logarithmic divergence (from
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
multiplying the Wick square).
This is absorbed by shifting the mass counterterm:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}C{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6}\|{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}\|_{L^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}(\text{log }N\text{ correction})

The precise counterterm shift is determined by the regularization scheme;
see Hairer (2014, Section 9) or Gubinelli-Imkeller-Perkowski (2015,
Proposition 6.3) for the explicit formula. For the present argument, only
the finiteness of the renormalized difference matters.

After renormalization, V(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
) - V(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
) is a well-defined random variable
under
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(and under
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
). The dominant fluctuation term is 4 int
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
: dx,
which has the right regularity:

•

:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
: in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}}(T^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}})
(as a distribution)

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}C^{\infty}(T^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
(smooth)

•

int
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
: dx is well-defined (pairing of smooth test function with distribution)

11.3.5
5. Integrability and equivalence

The Radon-Nikodym derivative
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{d}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}/d{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
involves:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(-(V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})-V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}l_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})-\text{const})

We need
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}L^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu})
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
a.s.

Positivity:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
a.s. because it's an exponential.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\checkmark

Integrability:
The critical term is exp(4 int
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
: dx) under the

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
measure. By the log-Sobolev inequality for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
(Barashkov-Gubinelli
2020), the measure has strong concentration: the tails of int
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
: dx
are controlled by the quartic interaction. Specifically:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}E_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(t|{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}:\mathrm{d}x|)\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}\infty\text{for}|t|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}t_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
depends on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\|{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}\|_{C^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}
and the coupling constant.
This exponential integrability follows from the coercivity of the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}

interaction: the quartic potential dominates the cubic perturbation
(Barashkov-Gubinelli 2020, Section 4, exponential integrability from the
Polchinski flow). The bound suffices for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}L^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu})
since the exponent
in the Radon-Nikodym derivative is bounded by 4
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\|{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}\|_{C^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}}|{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}:{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}:\,\mathrm{d}x|,

and this yields integrability provided the coefficient lies in the admissible
exponential-moment range (or after a standard scaling/localization argument).

Therefore
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}L^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu})
and 1/
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}L^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}),
giving:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}~{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}(\text{equivalent measures})

11.3.6
6. Alternative argument via the variational approach

Barashkov-Gubinelli (2020) construct the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
measure via the Boué-Dupuis
variational formula, which represents:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}-{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\log}Z\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\inf_{u}E\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}V({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}u_{s}\mathrm{d}s){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}||u_{s}||^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrm{d}s\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

In this framework, shifting by
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
is equivalent to modifying the variational
problem by a shift in the drift, which produces an equivalent measure (the
infimum shifts by a finite amount, preserving absolute continuity).

11.3.7
7. Summary

The measures are equivalent because:

1.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
~
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(interacting measure equivalent to Gaussian, by positivity of density)

2.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}
~
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(Cameron-Martin theorem, since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}H^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
)

3.

V(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
) - V(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
) is well-defined after renormalization

4.

The exponential of the cubic perturbation is integrable (log-Sobolev / coercivity)

5.

Therefore
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
~
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}
