Chapter 12
Problem 2: Universal Test Vector for Rankin-Selberg Integrals

12.1
Problem Statement

Let F be a non-archimedean local field with ring of integers o. Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{r}

denote the subgroup of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{r}(F)
consisting of upper-triangular unipotent
elements. Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathbb{C}^{x}
be a nontrivial additive character of conductor o,
identified with a generic character of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{r}
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
be a generic irreducible admissible representation of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(F)
,
realized in its
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
-Whittaker model
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})
. Must there exist

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})
with the following property?

For any generic irreducible admissible representation
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
in its

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
-Whittaker model
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})
, let q be the conductor ideal of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},Q{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F^{{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}}a
generator of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
:=
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}QE_{n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}.
Then for
some
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})
, the local Rankin-Selberg
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}_{N_{n}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}W(\operatorname{diag}(g,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})u_{Q})V(g)|{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}g|^{s-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrm{d}g

is finite and nonzero for all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}s{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}C
.

12.2
Answer

Yes.
The new vector (essential Whittaker function) of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
serves as a
universal test vector, with the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
twist compensating for the conductor of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
.

Process Note — Confidence

Confidence: Medium-high.
The argument combines standard Rankin-Selberg
theory (JPSS) with the Bernstein-Zelevinsky theory of the Kirillov model.
The key nondegeneracy claim (Section 3a) uses
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-equivariance of the

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\zeta}-{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
pairing together with irreducibility of the Kirillov model
(any nonzero function generates the full module under
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-translates)
and the PID structure of the fractional ideal ring.
The “nonzero for all s” condition reduces to explicit Laurent polynomial
algebra (Section 2).

12.3
Solution

12.3.1
1. Rankin-Selberg theory background

The local Rankin-Selberg
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}I(s,W,V)
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
is:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I(s,W,V)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}_{N_{n}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}W(\operatorname{diag}(g,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}))V(g)|{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}g|^{s-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrm{d}g

This converges for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Re(s)\gg{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
and extends to a rational function of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q_{F}^{-s}

(where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q_{F}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
|o/p|). The set of all such integrals as
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(W,V)
vary generates
a fractional ideal of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
, whose generator is the local
L-factor
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})
.

The problem modifies this by inserting
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}QE_{n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
into the
argument of W, giving the “twisted” Rankin-Selberg
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
.

12.3.2
2. The condition “finite and nonzero for all s”

For a rational function
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}f(q_{F}^{-s})
to be
finite and nonzero for all $s \in C$,

it must have no poles and no zeros when viewed as a function of

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}q_{F}^{-s}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathbb{C}^{x}
. Such a rational function must be
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}X^{k}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}q_{F}^{-ks}

for some nonzero c and integer k.

So the condition requires: there exists
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V
such that
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I(s,W,V)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}q_{F}^{-ks}

for some nonzero constant c and integer k.

By Section 3a below, the integrals over
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V
(for fixed
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
) generate the
full fractional ideal
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})\,{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\,\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
.

Explicit algebra:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I
is a free rank-1 module over the ring

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
, generated by
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})
. We seek an element
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I
of the form
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}q_{F}^{-ks}
(a monomial — no poles or zeros). Write:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}q_{F}^{-ks}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}P(q_{F}^{-s})

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}q_{F}^{-ks}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}.
Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}

is a polynomial in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q_{F}^{-s}
(the local L-factor for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
is a
product of terms (1 -
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{i}q_{F}^{-s})^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},
so its reciprocal is a
polynomial), P is a Laurent polynomial in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q_{F}^{-s}
— hence
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R
.
Therefore
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}q_{F}^{-ks}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}I
, and by the spanning property, some
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})
realizes this element as its Rankin-Selberg
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
against
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.

12.3.3
3. The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
twist and the Kirillov model

The key role of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
: right-translating W by
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
gives a new Whittaker function

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R(u_{Q})W{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})
. The restriction to the mirabolic subgroup

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
gives the Kirillov model, and the function:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}(g):\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W(\operatorname{diag}(g,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})u_{Q})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(R(u_{Q})W)(\operatorname{diag}(g,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}))

lies in the Kirillov model of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
restricted to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
.

We need
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}
to be nonzero as a function on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. This requires two
separate facts:

Fact 1:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R(u_{Q})W
is nonzero in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})
.
Right translation by

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
preserves the Whittaker model (since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
lies in the unipotent radical
of the standard maximal parabolic, which normalizes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
). The map

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}R(u_{Q})W
is a linear automorphism of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})
(with inverse
R(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
)), so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R(u_{Q})W\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
whenever W
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\neq
0.

Fact 2:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}(g)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(\operatorname{diag}(g,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})u_{Q})
is nonzero for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
new vector.

This is verified by direct evaluation at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I_{n}
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}(I_{n})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(\operatorname{diag}(I_{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})\cdot u_{Q})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(u_{Q})

Now
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}QE_{n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
is an element of the upper-triangular
unipotent subgroup
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
(its only off-diagonal entry is Q in position

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
). By the Whittaker-model transformation rule:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(u_{Q})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(u_{Q})\cdot W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(I_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})

The generic character
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
evaluates on superdiagonal entries:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}(u_{Q})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}(Q)
(the only superdiagonal entry of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
is Q in position

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
). With the standard normalization
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(I_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}(I_{n})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(Q)\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(Q)

Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}
is a nontrivial character of F and Q
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\neq
0 (Q generates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}),{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(Q)
is a nonzero complex number (it lies on the unit circle).
Therefore
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}(I_{n})\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},
so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}
is nonzero as a function on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\square

(Note: this argument is specific to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
new vector with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(I)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
.
For the universality claim we only need
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}
nonzero for this particular

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, so the direct computation suffices.)

Revision — Closing the Universality Gap

The standard JPSS nondegeneracy result (Section 2.7) states that as BOTH W
and V vary, the integrals
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I(s,W,V)
generate the full fractional ideal

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
. For the universality claim, we need
this to hold for FIXED
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
. This requires the following:

Lemma (fixed-W spanning).
Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
be a generic irreducible admissible
representation of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(F)
, and let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
be any nonzero function in the
Kirillov model of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
restricted to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. Then the set of integrals

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}_{N_{n}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}(g)V(g)|{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}g|^{s-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrm{d}g:V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})}

generates a nonzero fractional ideal of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
(contained in
the full ideal
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
).

Proof sketch.
The Kirillov model of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}|_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}
contains all compactly
supported locally constant functions on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{n}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
(Bernstein-Zelevinsky
1976, Theorem 5.21 and Corollary 5.22). In particular,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
can be
approximated by compactly supported functions. The Rankin-Selberg
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}

against a fixed nonzero
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
defines a nonzero linear functional on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})

(as a function of V), since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
is generic and hence
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})
separates
points on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{n}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
. The resulting set of integrals is a nonzero

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
-submodule of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
.

Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
is a PID (a Laurent polynomial ring in one variable

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q_{F}^{-s}
), every nonzero submodule of a free rank-1 module is itself free of
rank 1. Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}(s){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
be this submodule, where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

divides
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})
.

To show
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})
(i.e., fixed
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
generates the FULL ideal):

The JPSS theory (1983, Section 2.7) shows that the full ideal is generated
by letting both W and V vary. Varying W (with restriction to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
)
corresponds to varying
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
in the full Kirillov model
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi})|_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}
. The
ideal generated by ALL
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}R
. We must show that a
SINGLE nonzero
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
already suffices.

Consider the map
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}:K({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi})|_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}(\text{fractional ideals of }R)
defined by

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}I(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi},V):V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}){\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}\cdot R
. By the JPSS theory,

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})
generates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R. Since R is a PID,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R for some
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}
dividing
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})
.

Key step.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}
is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-equivariant in the following sense: for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, the substitution
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}g\cdot g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
in the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
gives

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I(s,R(g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi},V)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}|{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-s}\cdot I(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi},R^{\prime}(g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})V),

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R^{\prime}
denotes the contragredient action on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})
. Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R^{\prime}(g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
is an
automorphism of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})
, the set of integrals

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}I(s,R(g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi},V):V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}){\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}|{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-s}\cdot I(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi},V):V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}){\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}|{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-s}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}).

But
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-s}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}q_{F}^{-k\cdot s}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot

(unit in R) is a unit in the localization, so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}(R(g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})\,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})

generate the same fractional ideal. That is:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{R(g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

(up to units in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R
) for all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
.

Now, the Kirillov model
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi})|_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}
is irreducible as a
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}-

representation (Bernstein-Zelevinsky 1976, Theorem 5.21). Therefore
for any nonzero
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
, the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-translates
{

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R(g_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{GL}_{n}

}

span all of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi})|_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}
. We use this in two directions:

Direction 1 (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}
divides
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})
):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\subseteq}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{full}

(the full ideal generated by all W and V), so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\subseteq}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R.
Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\subseteq}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R, we get
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}
divides
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})

in the PID R (in a PID, (a)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\subseteq}
(b) iff b | a).

Direction 2 (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})
divides
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}
):
For any nonzero
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}K({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi})|_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}
, write
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\prime}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}c_{i}R(g_{i}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}
(finite linear combination
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-translates, possible by irreducibility). For each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\prime},V)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}c_{i}\cdot I(s,R(g_{i}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi},V)

By the equivariance in the key step, I(s,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R(g_{i}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi},
V) lies in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R
for each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}i
(since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}(R(g_{i})\,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{R(g_{i}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R). The sum
of elements of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R lies in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R (it's a module). Therefore

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\prime})

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\subseteq}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R for every nonzero
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\prime}
.

The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R
-submodule generated by all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}^{\prime})
equals the full Rankin-Selberg
ideal
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\subseteq}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cdot
R. So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}|L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}).

Combining both directions:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})
(up to units in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R
).
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\square

Application:
Taking
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}
(the restriction of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R(u_{Q})W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, which is
nonzero by the Kirillov injectivity lemma), the integrals over
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V
generate the
full fractional ideal for our fixed
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
. This closes the universality gap
identified by the reviewer.

12.3.4
4. Nondegeneracy of the pairing

For a nonzero
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}
in the Kirillov model, the Rankin-Selberg pairing:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V|{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}_{N_{n}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}(g)V(g)|{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}g|^{s-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrm{d}g

is nondegenerate as V ranges over
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})
. This is the fundamental
nondegeneracy of the Rankin-Selberg
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
(Jacquet-Piatetski-Shapiro-
Shalika 1983, Section 2.7).

More precisely: since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}
is nonzero, the integrals over all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})

generate the full fractional ideal
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
.

12.3.5
5. Choosing W: the new vector

Choice:
Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
be the new vector (essential Whittaker function) of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
,
i.e., the vector in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})
fixed by the congruence subgroup

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(p^{c({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi})})
where c(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
) is the conductor exponent of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
.

Properties of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
:

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
is nonzero (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
is generic)

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(I_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
(standard normalization)

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R(u_{Q})W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
is nonzero for every
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Q{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F^{{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}}
(as argued in Step 3)

Universality:
For any generic
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
with conductor ideal q:

•

Let Q generate
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}

•

The function
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\phi}_{Q}(g)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(\operatorname{diag}(g,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})u_{Q})
is nonzero in the Kirillov model

•

By the nondegeneracy (Step 4), there exists
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})
such that

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I(s,W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},V)
is a nonzero element of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathbb{C}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}q_{F}^{s},q_{F}^{-s}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

•

By the algebraic argument (Step 2), V can be chosen to make the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}

equal to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}q_{F}^{-ks}
, which is finite and nonzero for all s

12.3.6
6. The role of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
in conductor matching

The insertion of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}QE_{n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
is essential: it compensates for the
conductor of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
. Without this twist, for highly ramified
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
, the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}

might degenerate (the new vector of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
would not “see” the ramification of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
).

Support analysis via the Casselman-Shalika formula.
By the
Casselman-Shalika formula (Shintani 1976, Casselman-Shalika 1980), the
new vector
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
has support contained in

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}T^{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}K_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(p^{c({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi})})

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T^{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}
is the dominant cone in the diagonal torus. Right translation by

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}QE_{n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
shifts the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
-matrix entry by
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{F}^{-c({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})}.

In the Iwasawa decomposition
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}nak
, this modifies the diagonal component
at scale Q. Concretely, the support of the twisted function

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g|{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(\operatorname{diag}(g,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})u_{Q})

intersects the support of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(p^{c({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})})-fixed
vectors in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\psi})

nontrivially, because the Q-shift matches the conductor scale of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
exactly:
the new vector of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
is supported on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}T^{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}K_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(p^{c({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})})
(by the
analogous Casselman-Shalika result for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
), and the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
twist aligns the
two conductor levels (see Jacquet-Piatetski-Shapiro-Shalika 1981, Section 5,
for the precise support computation in the Rankin-Selberg setting).

This support matching is what makes the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
nondegenerate: it ensures
that the integrand
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(\operatorname{diag}(g,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})u_{Q}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}V(g)
is not identically zero on

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{n}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
for appropriate V.

12.3.7
7. Verification in special cases

Both unramified (c(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}c({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}E_{n,n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(Q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}).W_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}
is the spherical vector. The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(spherical for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
)
gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L(s,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}(\text{correction factor})
. Choose V to cancel the L-factor.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
unramified,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
ramified:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
has
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{F}^{-c({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi})}.
The twist
shifts the support of the spherical vector to match the ramification of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
.
This is the classical “conductor-lowering” mechanism.

Both ramified:
The new vector of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
combined with the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
twist gives a
function in the Kirillov model whose support is compatible with the
conductor of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
. Nondegeneracy follows from the general JPSS theory.

12.3.8
8. Summary

1.

The answer is YES: the new vector
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Pi}
is a universal test vector

2.

For any
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
with conductor q, the twist
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{Q}
(Q generates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
) ensures
the Rankin-Selberg pairing is nondegenerate

3.

Nondegeneracy of the Kirillov restriction:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R(u_{Q})W_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
is always nonzero

4.

The fractional ideal structure of Rankin-Selberg integrals allows
choosing V to make the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
a monomial
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}q_{F}^{-ks}

5.

This monomial is finite and nonzero for all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}s{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}C

12.4

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F
