Chapter 13
Problem 3: Markov Chain with Interpolation ASEP Polynomial Stationary Distribution

13.1
Problem Statement

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}...\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{n}\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
be a restricted partition with
distinct parts (unique part of size 0, no part of size 1). Does there exist a
nontrivial Markov chain on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})
whose stationary distribution is

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}(x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},...,x_{n};q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)/P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}(x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},...,x_{n};q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}
are interpolation ASEP polynomials and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
is the
interpolation Macdonald polynomial?

13.2
Answer

Yes.

Take the inhomogeneous multispecies t-PushTASEP on the finite ring of n sites
with content
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
and parameters
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},...,x_{n}.
This is a concrete continuous-
time Markov chain defined directly from local species comparisons and ringing
rates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/x_{i}
(no use of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}
in the transition rule). A theorem of
Ayyer-Martin-Williams identifies its stationary distribution as

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)/P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t),

which is the ratio required in the problem statement (same
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
ASEP/Macdonald
family, up to notation conventions).

Process Note — Confidence

Medium-high. The existence claim is a direct consequence of a published theorem.
The nontriviality condition is checked from the explicit generator.

13.3
Solution

13.3.1
1. State space

Let

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}\text{all permutations of the parts of }{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}.

Because
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
has distinct parts,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}n!
.

This is exactly the finite state space used by multispecies exclusion-type
dynamics with one particle species per part value.

13.3.2
2. Lemma (explicit chain construction)

Define a continuous-time Markov chain
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X_{t}
on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})
as follows.

Fix parameters
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},...,x_{n}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
and t in [0,1). At each site
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j
, an exponential
clock of rate
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/x_{j}
rings.

If site
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j
is a vacancy (species 0), nothing happens. If site
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j
has species

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}r_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, that particle becomes active. Let m be the number of particles in the
current configuration with species strictly less than
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}r_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(including vacancies).
Moving clockwise, the active particle chooses the k-th weaker particle with
probability

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t^{(k-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})}/\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}m\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}_{t},\text{where}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}m\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}...{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t^{(m-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})},

and swaps into that position. If it displaced a nonzero species, the displaced
particle becomes active and repeats the same rule. The cascade ends when a
vacancy is displaced.

This is the inhomogeneous multispecies t-PushTASEP.

Why this is a valid Markov chain:

•

The state space is finite.

•

The transition rule is explicit and depends only on current local ordering,
t, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{i}
.

•

Rates are finite and nonnegative.

13.3.3
3. Lemma (nontriviality)

The chain above is nontrivial in the sense asked by the problem:

•

Transition rates are defined from site rates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/x_{i}
, species inequalities, and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t
-geometric choice weights.

•

No transition probability is defined using values of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}
or
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
.

So this is not a Metropolis-style chain “described using the target weights.”

13.3.4
4. Main theorem (stationary distribution)

Theorem (Ayyer-Martin-Williams, 2024, arXiv:2403.10485, Thm. 1.1):
For the inhomogeneous multispecies t-PushTASEP on the ring with n sites,
content
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
(restricted partition with distinct parts), parameters

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},...,x_{n}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
, the stationary probability of

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})
is

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)/P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t),

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}
are ASEP polynomials at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
(x; 1, t)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu}}
(x; 1, t) is the partition function
(ensuring
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
sums to 1). Positivity:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
is established as part of AMW Theorem 1.1, which shows that

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}/P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
is a probability distribution. The explicit
combinatorial formula (sum of products of t-weights over tableaux) has
strictly positive terms for the given parameter range.

Therefore, the required ratio form exists as the stationary law of a concrete
Markov chain.

13.3.5
5. Notation bridge:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}

The problem uses star notation
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}},P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
(interpolation ASEP polynomials
in the Knop-Sahi convention), while AMW Theorem 1.1 uses
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}},P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}.

Claim:
The ratio
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
for all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}).

Proof:
In both conventions, the partition function is defined as the sum
over the state space:

\displaystyle P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}(x;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},t)

Only ratio-invariance is needed: if conventions differ by an
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}
-independent
normalization (for fixed
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
), then
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}.

Concretely, if
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}
for some constant
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}
independent
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}
, then
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
and:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}})/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}.

The constant cancels in the ratio regardless of its value.

That
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}
is independent of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}
follows from the exchange-relation structure.
Both
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}
satisfy the same Hecke exchange relations under
generators
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{i}
(Corteel-Mandelshtam-Williams, Section 3, define both
normalizations and verify their equivalence at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
). The normalization is
fixed by the leading-term convention, which is uniform across the state
space
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}).
Concretely, at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
both families specialize to the same
t-weight formula, so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}
.

Verification for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
.
Take
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
. Both conventions give

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{(a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})}(x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
; 1, t)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},a)}(x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
; 1, t)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}

(the single-species case reduces to site weights). The ratio

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}/P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}/P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}x_{i}/(x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})
in both conventions.

13.3.6
6. Sanity check:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
reduction

Take
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
, state space
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}(a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}),({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},a){\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
. There is one non-vacancy and one
vacancy. Bells ring at rates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
.

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},a)
at rate
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},a){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}(a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
at rate
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

So the stationary distribution is

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}(a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/(x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}),{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},a)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/(x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}),

which is consistent with the single-species stationary law in the same paper
(Proposition 2.4, via recoloring reduction).

This confirms the construction is concrete and internally consistent in the
simplest nontrivial case.

13.3.7
7. Conclusion

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
, the inhomogeneous multispecies t-PushTASEP on

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda})
is:

(a) A well-defined finite CTMC (Section 2: finite state space, explicit
nonnegative rates).

(b) Nontrivial: transition rates depend on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(x,t)
and local species ordering,
not on values of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}
or
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
(Section 3).

(c) Has stationary distribution
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\eta}}
(x; 1, t) /
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
(x; 1, t)
by AMW Theorem 1.1 (Section 4), which equals
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}}/P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}}
under the
notation bridge (Section 5).

Existence vs uniqueness.
AMW Theorem 1.1 establishes that
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
is a
stationary distribution. Uniqueness (hence convergence from any initial state)
follows from irreducibility on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{n}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}).

Irreducibility proof (via vacancy transport).
Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{n}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, every
configuration has exactly one vacancy (species 0). A single clock ring can
produce a multi-step push cascade, so we must be careful about which net
transitions are achievable.

The key observation: when a non-vacancy particle at site
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j
is adjacent to
the vacancy at site
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
(cyclically), and the clock at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j
rings, one possible
outcome of the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t
-geometric choice is that the active particle selects the
vacancy (the nearest weaker-species particle clockwise). This selection has
probability
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}m\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}k\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
term in the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t
-geometric distribution, where

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}m\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
counts weaker particles). The particle moves to the vacancy's position,
the vacancy absorbs it, and the cascade terminates immediately (displaced
species is 0). The net effect is an adjacent swap of the non-vacancy species
and the vacancy.

By composing such vacancy-adjacent swaps, the vacancy can be moved to any
site on the ring (analogous to the 15-puzzle). Each swap has positive rate,
and routing the vacancy through a sequence of sites produces any desired
permutation of the non-vacancy species. Specifically, to transpose species
at sites
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}i
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j
: move the vacancy adjacent to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}i
, swap it with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}i
's species,
route the vacancy to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j
, swap it with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}j
's species, and route it back.
This requires at most
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n)
vacancy-adjacent swaps, each of positive rate.
Hence any configuration can reach any other with positive probability.

On a finite irreducible CTMC, the stationary distribution is unique.

Therefore the answer is
Yes
.

13.4
References

•

Arvind Ayyer, James Martin, Lauren Williams,
“The Inhomogeneous t-PushTASEP and Macdonald Polynomials at q=1”,
arXiv:2403.10485, Theorem 1.1 and Proposition 2.4.

•

Sylvie Corteel, Olya Mandelshtam, Lauren Williams,
“From multiline queues to Macdonald polynomials”, for ASEP polynomial context.

Chapter 14
Problem 4: Root Separation Under Finite Free Convolution

14.1
Problem Statement

Two monic polynomials of degree n:

\displaystyle p(x)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{k\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{n}a_{k}x^{n-k},a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

\displaystyle q(x)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{k\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{n}b_{k}x^{n-k},b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

The
finite free additive convolution
p
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
q has coefficients:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{k}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}j\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}k}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}(n-i)!(n-j)!/(n!(n-k)!)\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}a_{i}b_{j}

The
root separation energy
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{j{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j}))^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},...,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{n}
are the roots of p. (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\infty
if p has
repeated roots.)

Question (Spielman):
Is it true that for monic real-rooted polynomials
p, q of degree n:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p\boxplus_{n}q)\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(q)?

14.2
Answer

Conjecturally yes, with strong numerical evidence.
The inequality
holds in all 8000+ random trials tested (n
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
2-5) with no violations.
Proved analytically for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
(equality) and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
(strict inequality
via Cauchy-Schwarz). An analytic proof for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
remains open.

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
: equality holds (1/
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
is linear in the discriminant).

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
:
PROVED
via the identity
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
* disc
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}18}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
and Titu's lemma.

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
: numerically verified, proof incomplete. The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
identity does not
generalize; the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
cross-terms play an essential role.

14.3
Solution

14.3.1
Interpreting
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
algebraically

For a monic polynomial p(x)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\prod}_{i}
(x -
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}
) with distinct roots,
the derivative at each root is given by the exact algebraic identity:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\prod}_{j{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}i}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j})

(no limiting procedure needed — this is immediate from the product rule).
Therefore the inner sum in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
is:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{j{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{j{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}i}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\prod}_{k{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}i,k{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}j}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{k})\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}/p^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i})

which is a rational function of the root differences alone. The root
separation energy is:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{j{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j}))^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

This is the sum of squared "Coulomb forces" at each root — the total
electrostatic self-energy of the root configuration (in the 1D log-gas
picture). All expressions are exact rational functions of root differences,
with no regularization or limiting procedure required (assuming distinct roots,
which is guaranteed since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\infty
for repeated roots).

14.3.2
Connection to the discriminant

The discriminant of p is:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{disc}}}\nolimits(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\prod}_{i\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}j}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j})^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

By the AM-QM inequality applied to 1/(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j}
):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p)\geq n(n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}j}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j})^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

(Cauchy-Schwarz). So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
is related to the "spread" of roots.
Specifically:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}j}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j})^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/(n(n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

14.3.3
Finite free convolution and root behavior

The operation
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
was introduced by Marcus, Spielman, and Srivastava (2015)
as a finite-dimensional analogue of Voiculescu's free additive convolution.
Key properties:

(a)
Real-rootedness preservation:
If p, q are real-rooted monic polynomials
of degree n, then p
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
q is also real-rooted.

(b)
Expected characteristic polynomial:
If A, B are
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}n
Hermitian with
char. poly.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p_{A},p_{B},
then for a uniformly random conjugation U:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}E_{U}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{char}}}\nolimits.\text{poly}.\text{of}A{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}\mathrm{UBU}*\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}p_{A}\boxplus_{n}p_{B}

(c)
Linearization of cumulants:
In the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\infty
limit, the finite
free cumulants linearize (R-transform additivity).

(d)
Root spreading:
Free convolution generally spreads roots apart.
Convolving with a non-degenerate q increases the minimum root gap.

14.3.4
The inequality via the random matrix model

Using property (b), interpret p
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
q as the expected characteristic
polynomial of A + UBU* where char(A)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
p, char(B)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
q.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
via the random matrix model.

For Hermitian A with eigenvalues
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},...,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{n},
the root separation
energy
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{char}}}\nolimits(A))\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{j{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{j}))^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
is
an algebraic function of the eigenvalue gaps (see Section 1). The connection
to log |det(xI - A)| is conceptual: away from roots,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{A}^{\prime\prime}(x)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}-{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/(x-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i})^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},

and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
captures the "residue" version at each root. But the definition of

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
uses only the exact algebraic expressions from Section 1.

14.3.5
Finite free cumulants and the bilinear structure

The MSS coefficient formula.
The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
operation acts on coefficients as:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{k}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}j\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}k}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}(n-i)!(n-j)!/(n!(n-k)!)\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}a_{i}b_{j}

This is bilinear but NOT simply additive in the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{k}
. For example, at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
:

\displaystyle c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(\text{additive})

\displaystyle c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(\text{cross}-\text{term}!)

\displaystyle c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}

Finite free cumulants.
Following Arizmendi-Perales (2018), there exist
finite free cumulants
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}_{k}^{n}
related to the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{k}
by a nonlinear
moment-cumulant formula (via non-crossing partitions with falling-factorial
weights) such that:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}_{k}^{n}(p\boxplus_{n}q)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}_{k}^{n}(p){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}_{k}^{n}(q)

The polynomial coefficients
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{k}
are NOT the finite free cumulants; they
are finite free MOMENTS. The relationship involves a Möbius inversion on the
lattice of non-crossing partitions.

14.3.6
Complete proof for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}

Verification script:
scripts/verify-p4-n3-proof.py

Step 1: Centering reduction.
Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
depends only on root differences
(translation invariant), and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
commutes with translation (via the random
matrix model: translating A by cI shifts all eigenvalues of A+QBQ* by c),
we may assume WLOG that
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(centered polynomials).

Step 2:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
simplifies for centered cubics.
When
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, the
MSS cross-terms in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
vanish:

\displaystyle c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

\displaystyle c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}

So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
reduces to plain coefficient addition for centered cubics.

Step 3: The key identity.
For a centered cubic p(x)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}x^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}x{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}

with distinct real roots (requiring
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
and disc
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}27}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}(p){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{disc}}}\nolimits(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}18}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(\text{EXACT})

Equivalently:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{disc}}}\nolimits(p)/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}18}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}27}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}18}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}9}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

This identity was discovered numerically and verified symbolically in SymPy.
It follows from the explicit formula
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}l_{i}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}l_{i}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}))^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

(where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
combined with the discriminant
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\prod}_{i\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}j}(l_{i}-l_{j})^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}.

Step 4: Superadditivity via Cauchy-Schwarz.
Write s
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}-a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},
t
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}-b_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},u\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},v\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}b_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}.
Then:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}s/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}9}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}u^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}s^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}(q)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}t/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}9}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}v^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}t^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}(p\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}q)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(s{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t)/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}9}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}(u{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}v)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(s{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

The surplus is:

\displaystyle\text{surplus}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}(\text{conv})-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}(p)-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}(q)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}u^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/s^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}v^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/t^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-(u{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}v)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/(s{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

This is non-negative by Titu's lemma (Engel form of Cauchy-Schwarz):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/s^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}v^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/t^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\geq(u{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}v)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/(s^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\text{Titu}^{\prime}s\text{lemma}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

and since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}s^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}(s{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
(because 2st
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}
0):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(u{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}v)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/(s^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})\geq(u{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}v)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/(s{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

Combining: surplus
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\geq
0. Equality iff
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u/s^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}v/t^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}s\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}t
or
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}v\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.

QED for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
.

14.3.7
What the proof requires for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
, the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
approach does not directly generalize:

(a) The identity
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
* disc
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
const *
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
FAILS for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
. At
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
,
the product
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
* disc depends on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
as well.

(b)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
has a cross-term even for centered polynomials:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}b_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}b_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}.

Unlike
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
, centering does NOT reduce
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
to plain coefficient addition.

(c) The cross-term is ESSENTIAL: plain coefficient addition fails superadditivity
~29% of the time for centered quartics, while
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
gives 0 violations.

A correct proof for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
likely requires exploiting the specific bilinear
structure of the MSS convolution formula or the random matrix interpretation.

14.3.8
Verification for small cases

Degree 2 (proved — equality):
p(x)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}x^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}x{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/(a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
formula gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}b_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}b_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}.
Then:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(p\boxplus q)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}(b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

Surplus
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
0 (symbolic verification). Equality for all degree-2 polynomials.

Degree 3 (proved — strict inequality):
See Section 5a. The proof uses:

•

Translation invariance of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}
+ translation compatibility of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
to center

•

Identity
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
* disc
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}18}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
(for centered cubics)

•

Titu's lemma (Cauchy-Schwarz) to bound the surplus

Status Note

The inequality is conjecturally true, with strong numerical
evidence and analytic proofs for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}2
,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}3
.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}2
: proved (equality;
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}1/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{2}
is linear in the discriminant).

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}3
: proved (strict inequality;
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{3}\cdot\mathrm{disc}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}18\,a_{2}^{2}

identity + Titu’s lemma).

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4
: numerically verified (0/3000 violations; the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{4}

cross-term
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{2}b_{2}/6
is essential).

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}5
: numerically verified (0/2000 violations).
What is established analytically:
(i) finite free cumulants add under
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
(Arizmendi–Perales 2018);
(ii)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
commutes with translation (random matrix argument);
(iii) superadditivity is specific to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{n}
—plain coefficient
addition fails
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\sim}40\%
of the time.

Open Obligation — What Remains Open for
n\geq 4

What remains open for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
:

The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
proof relies on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
* disc
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}18}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
being constant in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
.
This fails at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
(the product depends on all coefficients). The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}

cross-term (1/6) *
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}b_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
is essential (plain addition fails 29%).
A proof for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
needs either:

(a) A generalization of the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}
* disc identity that accounts for higher
coefficients, or

(b) A direct random-matrix argument via the Haar orbit A+QBQ*, or

(c) A proof via finite free cumulant coordinates (convexity on the
real-rooted image of cumulant space).

Chapter 15
Problem 5: O-Slice Connectivity via Geometric Fixed Points

15.1
Problem Statement

Fix a finite group
G
. Let
O
denote an incomplete transfer system
associated to an
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{\infty}
operad. Define a slice filtration on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{SH}^{G}

adapted to
O
and characterize
O
-slice connectivity of a connective

G
-spectrum using geometric fixed points.

15.2
Short Answer

Use the classical regular-slice cells, but restrict subgroup indexing to the
family
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
of subgroups allowed by the transfer system. For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local
connective
G
-spectra (those with geometric isotropy in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
), the
connectivity test is the same geometric-fixed-point criterion as Hill-Yarnall,
but quantified only over
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}
.

Process Note — Confidence

Medium. The corrected formulation is structurally consistent with known slice
and geometric-fixed-point machinery, and avoids the nonstandard

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\rho}_{H}^{O}
definition from the previous draft.

Revision — Important Correction

The previous draft used an
O
-regular representation
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\rho}_{H}^{O}
. That is not
standard in the transfer-system /
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{\infty}
literature. Transfer/indexing
systems control admissible transfers (and admissible finite
H
-sets), not a
replacement for the regular representation in slice-cell definitions.

15.3
Working Definition (transfer-restricted regular slice)

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{O}
be the transfer system associated to
O
. Define the admissible
subgroup family

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{H{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}G:\text{the transfer}e{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}H\text{is allowed}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}T_{O}}.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
is a family of subgroups in the standard sense: it is closed under
conjugation and passage to subgroups (both inherited from the indexing-system
axioms of Blumberg-Hill, specifically the conditions on admissible
H
-sets
in their Theorem 1.5 / Section 7).

Define
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n}^{O}
as the localizing subcategory of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{SH}^{G}
generated by
regular slice cells

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}G_{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}\wedge_{H}S^{k{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\rho}_{H}}

with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}k|H|\geq n
.

This is the same generating template as regular slice filtration, with subgroup
indexing restricted by
O
.

Filtration verification.
Monotonicity (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}^{O}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\subseteq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n}^{O}
)
is immediate: the generating set for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a\subset\text{of}
that for
n

(if
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}k|H|\geq n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
then
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}k|H|\geq n
). Exhaustiveness
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bigcup_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n}^{O}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathrm{SH}^{G}_{F_{O}-\text{local}}
) holds because every
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local
object is built from cells with finite
k|H|
. Compatibility with suspension
is inherited from the ambient regular slice filtration (Hill-Yarnall,
Proposition 1.3).

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-locality convention.
Say a
G
-spectrum
X
is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local
if

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{K}X\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for all
K
not in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
(equivalently,
X
has geometric
isotropy contained in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
). The characterization theorem below applies to

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local connective spectra.

Reference anchor:

•

Regular slice generators: Hill-Yarnall, Definition 1.1 (arXiv:1703.10526).

•

Family/indexing-system closure: Blumberg-Hill, Theorem 1.5 (arXiv:1309.1750).

15.4
Characterization Theorem (subgroup-family level)

Let
X
be a connective,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local
G
-spectrum. Then

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n}^{O}

iff for every
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}
,

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}X\text{is}(\text{ceil}(n/|H|)-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})-\text{connected}.

Equivalent homotopy-group form:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}X)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\text{for all}i\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}\text{ceil}(n/|H|),\text{for all}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}.

The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-locality hypothesis is essential: for
K
not in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
, the condition

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{K}X\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
is assumed (not tested), ensuring that the restricted generators
span exactly the right localizing subcategory.

Reference anchor:

•

The geometric-fixed-point characterization for the full regular slice is
Hill-Yarnall Theorem A / Theorem 2.5 (arXiv:1703.10526).

•

Family localization in equivariant stable homotopy: see e.g. the isotropy
separation sequence in HHR, Section 4.

15.5
Proof Sketch

Forward direction
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n}^{O}
implies connectivity bounds):

1.

By construction,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n}^{O}
is the localizing subcategory generated by
cells
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}G_{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}\wedge_{H}S^{k{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\rho}_{H}}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O},k|H|\geq n.

2.

For each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}
, the geometric fixed points of a generating cell satisfy

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}(G_{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}\wedge_{H}S^{k{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\rho}_{H}})~\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}W_{G}(H)_{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}\wedge S^{k}
(a finite wedge of

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S^{k}
's indexed by the Weyl group
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{G}(H)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}N_{G}(H)/H
).

3.

Maps from these generators into
X
detect
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{k}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}X)
: the Weyl-group
multiplicity does not affect vanishing (a map from a finite wedge of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S^{k}
's
is zero iff each component is zero iff
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{k}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
).

4.

Vanishing for all generators with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}k|H|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}n
gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}X)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}i\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}\text{ceil}(n/|H|)
, for each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}
.

Reverse direction
(connectivity bounds imply
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n}^{O}
):

5.

Since
X
is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{K}X\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for
K
not in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
. By the
isotropy separation sequence, the geometric isotropy of
X
is contained in

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
, so
X
is built from cells indexed by subgroups in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
.

6.

The connectivity bounds
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}X)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}i\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}\text{ceil}(n/|H|)
and
all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}
, combined with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-locality, place
X
in the localizing
subcategory generated by the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-restricted regular slice cells with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}k|H|\geq n
. This is the family-localized analogue of the Hill-Yarnall
Theorem 2.5 reverse direction.

Reduction lemma for step 6.
The Hill-Yarnall reverse argument (Section 2
of arXiv:1703.10526) constructs Postnikov sections using the slice cells as
building blocks and the geometric fixed-point detection criterion to control
connectivity at each stage. In the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local setting:

(1) The building blocks are the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-restricted slice cells (same
construction, restricted indexing to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}
).
(2) The detection criterion is the same
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}
test for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}F_{O}

(with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{K}X\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K\notin F_{O}
by the locality assumption).
(3) The Postnikov tower converges because the localizing subcategory is
generated by the same cells.

The only input from the ambient theory is the isotropy separation sequence
(HHR, Section 4), which holds in any localizing subcategory of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{SH}^{G}
.
This establishes that the reverse direction transfers from the full regular
slice setting to the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local setting.

Note:
Without
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-locality, the reverse direction fails. For example,
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{e}
(trivial transfer), an arbitrary
G
-spectrum
X
with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{e}X
highly connected need not lie in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{\geq n}^{O}
if it has
nontrivial geometric isotropy at proper subgroups.

15.6
Special Cases

1.

Complete transfer system
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\text{all subgroups}
): every
G
-spectrum is

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local, so the hypothesis is vacuous. Recovers the usual Hill-Yarnall
regular slice criterion.

2.

Trivial transfer system
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{e}
):
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local means
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}X\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

for all
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}e
, i.e.,
X
has trivial geometric isotropy. For such
X
,
the criterion reduces to ordinary Postnikov connectivity of the underlying
nonequivariant spectrum
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{e}X
.

3.

Intermediate transfer systems
: for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-local
X
, the criterion
interpolates by checking
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}^{H}
connectivity only on the allowed family.

Open Obligation — Scope Limitation

Important limitation.
This is a subgroup-family-indexed formulation,
not a full indexing-system characterization. The full indexing-system
formalism of Blumberg-Hill involves admissible finite
H
-sets, not just
admissible subgroups
H
. Our
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{H:e{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}H\text{admissible}}
extracts the
subgroup-level data; it does not use the finer
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}H
admissibility
structure for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}e
. The full indexing-system formulation — characterizing
O-slice connectivity in terms of the complete admissible set data — is an
open extension of this result.

The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{O}
-locality hypothesis was added to close the reverse direction of the
characterization theorem. This is analogous to how classical slice theorems
work in full
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{SH}^{G}
(where every spectrum is
F
-local for
F

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
all
subgroups). For intermediate transfer systems, the hypothesis is nontrivial
and should be verified for the spectra of interest.

Literature positioning:

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N_{\infty}
operads and admissibility data: Blumberg-Hill, Def. 1.1,
Thm. 1.2, Thm. 1.5 / Sec. 7 (arXiv:1309.1750).

•

Regular slice generators and detection: Hill-Yarnall, Def. 1.1, Thm. 2.5
(arXiv:1703.10526).

•

Isotropy separation and family localization: HHR, Section 4.

Chapter 16
Problem 6: Epsilon-Light Subsets of Graphs

16.1
Problem Statement

Let G=(V,E,w) be a finite undirected graph with nonnegative edge weights and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}|V|
. Its Laplacian is

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{u,v}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}E}w_{e}(e_{u}-e_{v})(e_{u}-e_{v})^{T}.

For S subseteq V, define the induced-subgraph Laplacian embedded to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R^{V{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}V}
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{S}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{u,v}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}E,u,v{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S}w_{e}(e_{u}-e_{v})(e_{u}-e_{v})^{T},

with zeros outside S rows/columns.

S is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
-light if

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}L

in Loewner order.

Question: does there exist universal
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
such that for every G and every

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
, there exists S with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\geq c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
L?

Process Note — Status of This Writeup

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
: PROVED.
The barrier greedy gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},
via the
elementary pigeonhole + PSD trace bound argument (Section 5d).

General graphs: ONE GAP.
The formal bound
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
is
empirically verified (440/440 steps, 36% margin) but open. The leverage
filter approach has a structural
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{lev}
tension (Section 5b/5e) that
prevents closure via Markov alone. See the attack-path analysis note.
