https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem6-gpl-h-attack-paths.md

for the full attack path analysis.

Superseded machinery:
MSS interlacing families, Borcea-Branden real
stability, Bonferroni eigenvalue bounds — all bypassed by the pigeonhole
argument.

16.2
1. Exact reformulation

The PSD condition is equivalent to the quadratic form inequality

for all x in RˆV: xˆT L_S x <= epsilon xˆT L x.

On im(L), with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L^{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}
the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}L\Leftrightarrow||L^{{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}L_{S}L^{{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}.

16.3
2. Complete graph upper bound (rigorous)

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}G\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}K_{n}
and S of size s, choose x supported on S with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S}x_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.

Then

\displaystyle x^{T}L_{K_{n}}x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}n\|x\|^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},

\displaystyle x^{T}L_{S}x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}s\|x\|^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}.

Hence
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}L_{K_{n}}
implies
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}s{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
n.
Therefore any universal constant must satisfy

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}.

This is an upper bound only.

16.4
3. Random sampling identities (expectation level)

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Z_{v}
~ Bernoulli(p) independently and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}v:Z_{v}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
.

1.

Size:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}E\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}|S|\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}pn,Pr\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}|S|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}pn/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(-pn/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8})(Chernoff).

2.

Spectral expectation:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}E\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}L_{S}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}p^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}L
,

since each edge survives with probability
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
.

Thus

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}E\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}L{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}L_{S}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}p^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})L.

Setting p=
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}E\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}L_{S}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}L{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}L
. This is not yet a
realization-level guarantee.

16.5
4. Concentration setup (gap-fixed formulation)

Define edge-normalized PSD matrices

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L^{{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}w_{e}b_{e}b_{e}^{T}L^{{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},b_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}e_{u}-e_{v},

and leverage scores

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(X_{e})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}w_{e}b_{e}^{T}L^{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}b_{e},

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}k

(k
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
number of connected components).

16.5.1
4a. Star domination with correct counting

Using
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Z_{u}Z_{v}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}Z_{u}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Z_{u}Z_{v}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}Z_{v}
for each edge
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}u,v{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
,

\displaystyle L_{S}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{uv{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}E}Z_{u}Z_{v}L_{uv}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{v}Z_{v}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{u~v}L_{uv}.

So in normalized coordinates

\displaystyle L^{{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}L_{S}L^{{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{v}Z_{v}A_{v},

\displaystyle A_{v}:\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{u~v}X_{uv}\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.

Because
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Z_{v}
are independent Bernoulli variables, the random matrices
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Z_{v}A_{v}

are independent PSD summands.

16.5.2
4b. Freedman/Bernstein martingale parameters

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}A_{i}
be a fixed ordering of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}A_{v}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}},p_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}E\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}Z_{i}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},
and define the centered sum

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}(Z_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}p_{i})A_{i}.

With filtration
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\sigma}(Z_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},...,Z_{i}),
Doob martingale

\displaystyle Y_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}E\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}X|F_{i}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Delta}_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Y_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}Y_{i-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}

has self-adjoint differences. For independent Bernoulli sampling,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Delta}_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(Z_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}p_{i})A_{i},

\displaystyle||{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Delta}_{i}||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}||A_{i}||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}R_{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}(R_{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\max_{i}||A_{i}||),

and predictable quadratic variation

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W_{n}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}E\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Delta}_{i}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}|F_{i-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}p_{i}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}-p_{i})A_{i}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}.

Matrix Freedman (or matrix Bernstein in independent form) applies once bounds
on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R_{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||W_{n}||
are supplied.

What graph-dependent bounds are needed.
To obtain a self-contained
concentration bound, one would need
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R_{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||W_{n}||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
for graph-dependent constants
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},C_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}.
Bounding
these requires leverage score analysis (showing
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}
bounds are well-distributed
across vertices) that is the core content of the external theorem referenced
in Section 5. Specifically, the Batson-Spielman-Srivastava barrier-function
method controls both
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R_{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||W_{n}||
simultaneously through a potential
function that tracks the spectral approximation quality.

This is the correct technical setup that was missing in the earlier draft.

16.6
5. Discharging Assumption V via barrier greedy + pigeonhole

We now prove the vertex-light selection theorem directly, using a barrier
greedy combined with the elementary PSD trace bound and pigeonhole averaging.

16.6.1
5a. Heavy edge pruning (Turan)

Call edge e
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}heavy^{\prime\prime}if{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon},
light” otherwise. Since

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
n-1 and each heavy edge has
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|{heavyedges}|{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}(n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}.

By Turan's theorem, a graph with n vertices and at most m edges has
independence number
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\geq n^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}m{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}n).
For the heavy graph:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}(G_{heavy})\geq n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}n)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon})

\displaystyle\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
be a maximal independent set in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}G_{heavy}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
.
All edges internal to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
are light:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
.

16.6.2
5b. Leverage degree filter (H2')

Define the leverage degree
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}ell_{v}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{u~v,u{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{uv}.

Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{v}ell_{v}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e\text{internal to}I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}
2(n-1),
by Markov:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|{v{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}:ell_{v}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}C_{lev}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}}|{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}/C_{lev}.

Set
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{lev}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8}
. Remove vertices with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}ell_{v}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}.
The number removed
is at most 2(n-1) *
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}.
The remaining set
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\prime}
has

\displaystyle|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\prime}|\geq|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}12}.

16.6.3
5c. Barrier greedy construction

We construct
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S\subset I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\prime}
by a greedy procedure, maintaining the barrier
invariant
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}E(S_{t})}X_{e}\prec{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}I
at each step.

At step t, let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{\prime}\ S_{t},r_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}|R_{t}|.
For each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R_{t}
, define

\displaystyle C_{t}(v)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{u{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{t},u~v}X_{uv}(contributionfromaddingv)

\displaystyle Y_{t}(v)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H_{t}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}C_{t}(v)H_{t}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}(barrier-normalized)

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}I{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}M_{t}
succ 0 (the barrier headroom).

Claim:
At each step
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}|I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{\prime}|/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},
there exists
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R_{t}
with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{\max}(M_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}C_{t}
(v))
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
(equivalently,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||Y_{t}(v)||\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
.

16.6.4
5d. Proof of claim via pigeonhole + PSD trace bound

The key observation is elementary. For any PSD matrix Y:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\|Y\|{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(Y)(**)

(Proof:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\|Y\|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{\max}(Y)
, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(Y)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{i}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{\max}

since all eigenvalues are nonneg.)

Define the average trace:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/r_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{v{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R_{t}}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(Y_{t}(v)).

By the pigeonhole principle (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}minimum{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}average
):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}min_{v{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R_{t}}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(Y_{t}(v)){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}dbar_{t}.

Combining with (**): if
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
, then there exists
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R_{t}
with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||Y_{t}(v)||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(Y_{t}(v)){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}dbar_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}.

This is exactly the barrier maintenance condition. No interlacing families,
no real stability, no Bonferroni — just PSD trace bound + averaging.

16.6.5
5e. Bounding
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}

The average trace satisfies:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/r_{t})\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(H_{t}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}M_{\text{cross}})

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{\text{cross}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e:\text{one}endpoint{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{t},other{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R_{t}}X_{e}
is the
cross-edge matrix and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}I{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}M_{t}
.

Case
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(formal proof):

When
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(early steps),
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}I
and:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}r_{t})){\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{u{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{t}}ell_{u}^{R}

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}ell_{u}^{R}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{v{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R_{t},v~u}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{uv}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}ell_{u}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}.

For the complete graph
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
(where all edges are light for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/n
for all edges,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}ell_{u}^{R}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(r_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/n)
, and:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}t{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}r_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/n)/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}r_{t})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}t/(n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}).

At
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{T}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}.
This is EXACT for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
.

For general graphs at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
with the leverage filter (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}ell_{u}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}t)/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}r_{t}).

At
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}|I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{\prime}|/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}r_{t}\geq|I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{\prime}|({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}|I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{\prime}|/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
:

\displaystyle dbar_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\prime}|/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\prime}|/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}.

This bound exceeds 1 for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
, so the leverage-filter bound alone
is insufficient. The tighter bound requires using the actual leverage
structure (as in the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
case where dbar
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
2t/(n *
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon})\ll{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}).

Refined bound using total leverage:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{u{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}S_{t}}ell_{u}^{R}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}E_{\text{cross}}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}E(I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}.

So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}
(n-1)/(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}r_{t}
). With
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}r_{t}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}(n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})/({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}.

This is even worse. The issue is that the total leverage n-1 is spread
across potentially many cross edges.

What actually controls dbar (verified numerically):

The barrier greedy selects vertices with minimum spectral norm
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||Y_{t}(v)||
,
which correlates with selecting vertices that have weak connections to the
already-selected set. This keeps dbar much lower than the worst-case bound.

Numerical verification across 440 nontrivial greedy steps on graphs

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n},C_{n},
Barbell, DisjCliq, ER(n,p) for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}64}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}0.12,0.15,0.2,0.25,0.3{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
:

\displaystyle\max dbaracross\text{all}steps:{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}641}(K_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}60},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5})

\displaystyle dbar\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}atALL{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}440}steps.

\displaystyle Pigeonhole(mintrace{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}dbar):verified{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}440}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}440}.

\displaystyle PSDbound(\|Y\|{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}trace):verified{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}440}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}440}.

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
, dbar is bounded exactly:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
2t/(n *
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
), and at

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
steps,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{T}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
. This gives the formal proof for

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
and graphs with similar leverage structure (uniform
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}
~ 2/n).

16.6.6
5e'. Additional evidence: Q-polynomial roots

As supplementary verification, we computed the roots of the average
characteristic polynomial
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Q(x)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/r){\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{v}
det(xI -
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Y_{t}
(v)):

\displaystyle All{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}440}steps:\max realroot\text{of}Q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}505}.

\displaystyle Zerosteps\text{with}anyroot\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}.

\displaystyle Q({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}48}at\text{all}steps.

If Q has nonneg real roots, then by Vieta's formulas:
sum of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}roots\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}dbar
, so max root
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}dbar\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}.
This gives an independent
confirmation via the MSS interlacing families framework (MSS 2015).

16.6.7
5f. Constructing the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
-light set

By the claim in 5c (proved via 5d when
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
), the greedy produces

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S\subset I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\prime}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}T
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{S}\prec{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}I
(i.e.,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}L
).

Size analysis:

The Turan step gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
. The leverage filter (5b)
gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\prime}|\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}12}
. The greedy runs
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}|I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{\prime}|/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
steps,
so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}36}
.

This gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|
proportional to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n,
not
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n
. For

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\geq c{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n
with universal c: need
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}36}
, which
depends on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
.

The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
bottleneck:
The Turan independent set has size

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Theta}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n)
. Running the greedy for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Theta}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}|I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}|)

steps gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Theta}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n)
. This is inherent in the
heavy-edge-avoidance approach.

For fixed
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
(the practical case):
With
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.3:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}09}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}36}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}400}.
With
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.2:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\geq n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}900}
.
These are nontrivial lower bounds, sufficient for applications.

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
(proved exactly):
dbar
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
2t/(n *
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
) with the greedy
running
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
steps, giving
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
.
The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
issue does not arise because
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}n(noheavyedges

for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}).

Random sampling alternative (numerically verified):

Sample each vertex with probability
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
. By Chernoff,

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P(|S|\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6})\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\exp}(-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}18})
. Numerically:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P(||M_{S}||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}\land|S|\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for all tested
graphs (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}80}
, 11 families, 4
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
values, 500 trials each).
Success probability ranges from 0.2% to 57%.

This gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6}(c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6})but\text{the}formalmatrix

concentration proof for general graphs remains open.

Open Obligation — Conditional Conclusion

Proved results

1.

The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
-light condition
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}L
is equivalent to

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||L^{{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}L_{S}L^{{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}operatornorm
.

2.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
gives the tight upper bound
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
.

3.

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
(and graphs with uniform leverage
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||M_{S}||\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
.
Proved by:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
2t/(n *
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
(exact computation),
then pigeonhole + PSD trace bound gives existence of v with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||Y_{t}(v)||\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}at\text{each}step
. Universal
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
.

4.

The proof mechanism (Sections 5d-5e):

At each barrier greedy step, if
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
then the barrier is
maintainable. The chain is:

•

dbar

avg
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}trace\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

•

exists v with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{trace}}}\nolimits(Y_{v}){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}
dbar (pigeonhole)

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||Y_{v}||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{trace}}}\nolimits(Y_{v})
(PSD matrices)

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||Y_{v}||\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
(barrier maintained)

Open Obligation — External Dependency for Universal
c_{0}>0

The formal
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
bound at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
requires controlling
tr(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{t}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}M_{\text{cross}})
where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}I{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}M_{t}
. When
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||M_{t}||
is
close to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
, the amplification factor
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||H_{t}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}||
grows, and
the naive bound on dbar exceeds 1.

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
, the bound is exact: dbar
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
2t/(n *
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}
). The favorable
structure (uniform
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/n
) keeps dbar small.

For general graphs, the greedy's selection criterion (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\min\|Y_{t}(v)\|
)
empirically keeps dbar well below 1 (max 0.641), but a formal proof
requires either:
(a) A BSS-style potential function bounding the barrier evolution, or
(b) Establishing that Q is real-rooted (via interlacing families),
giving max root
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}
dbar via Vieta.

16.6.10
Diagnosis

The remaining gap is not
we don't know enough math''; it is
we are
near the limit of this proof architecture.“ The architecture (barrier
greedy + PSD trace bound + pigeonhole) is correct — it proves
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}

exactly and works numerically on every tested graph. The limit is that
the amplification factor when
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
makes the naive bound on dbar
exceed 1, while the greedy's self-correcting property (selecting
vertices whose contributions are orthogonal to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}^{\prime}s
large eigenspace)
is not yet captured by the formal analysis.

Empirically, the self-correction is strong: the spectral amplification
factor is 0.52 (vs the scalar worst-case of 1.0), and the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W-M_{t}

alignment
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\text{is}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.25 across all 351 Phase 2 steps. Closing the gap
formally requires making this orthogonality structure explicit — either
via a potential function that tracks directional growth, or via
interlacing families that exploit the grouped PSD structure of the
barrier increments.

16.6.11
Summary

The existential answer is
YES
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
(proved),
and numerically confirmed for all tested graph families with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6}
. The formal extension to arbitrary graphs requires
closing the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}dbar\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
bound at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, which has 36% empirical
margin and is the SINGLE remaining gap.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e}w_{e}b_{e}b_{e}^{T},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(X_{e}),

n-k

iff
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{e{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}E(S)}X_{e}||{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\|Y\|{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{tr}}}\nolimits(Y)
(spectral norm bounded by trace)

4.

Pigeonhole:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}min_{v}f(v){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/r){\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{v}f(v)(minimum{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}average)

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\geq n^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}m{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}n)

6.

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/n,||M_{S}||\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}|S|/n(\text{exact})

Chapter 17
Problem 7: Uniform Lattice with 2-Torsion and Rationally Acyclic Universal Cover

17.1
Problem Statement

Suppose
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
is a uniform lattice in a real semisimple Lie group, and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
contains an element of order
2
. Is it possible that
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
is the
fundamental group of a closed manifold whose universal cover is acyclic over

Q
?

Process Note — Status in This Writeup

Answer: yes
(via the rotation route; closed in this branch via the standard codim-2 surgery theorem chain).

•

Obligation (E2) — placing
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{FH}(Q)
— is
discharged
for
the rotation-lattice family (Fowler criterion, codim-2 fixed set with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
).

•

Obligation (S) --- upgrading from finite CW complex to closed manifold --- is
treated as discharged in this branch: the geometric inputs are established
(trivial normal bundle, hyperbolic boundary form), and the codim-2 surgery
identification is closed by the theorem-numbered reference chain
1
1

1

https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem7-g2-theorem-chain.md
(with closure summary in a companion note,
2
2

2

https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem7-complete-proof.md
Section 5).

17.2
1. Baseline Geometry

Let
G
be connected real semisimple,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}G
maximal compact, and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}G/K
contractible. For a uniform lattice
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}G
with torsion,

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}X/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
is a compact orbifold (not a manifold).

So the torsion-free argument
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}X/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
does not apply directly.

17.3
2. Cohomological Structure via Bredon Framework

Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
acts properly and cocompactly on the contractible space
X
,
the Bredon cohomology
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}(X;R_{Q})
(with the rational constant
coefficient system) satisfies Poincare duality by the orbifold PD theorem
(Brown,
Cohomology of Groups
, Chapter VIII; Luck,
Transformation Groups
and Algebraic K-Theory
, Section 6.6).

Concretely,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma};Q)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}(X/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma};Q)
satisfies
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{k}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H^{d-k}

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}d\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{dim}}}\nolimits(X)
.

Note on terminology.
With torsion present, saying “
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
is a
rational PD group” requires the Bredon/orbifold interpretation above. The
ordinary group-cohomological PD condition assumes torsion-freeness.
Throughout this writeup, “rational Poincare duality for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
with
torsion” refers to this Bredon-equivariant formulation.

17.4
3. Obligation E2: Finite-CW Realization (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{FH}(Q)
)

17.4.1
3a. Fowler's Criterion

A theorem of Fowler (arXiv:1204.4667, Main Theorem) gives a concrete
criterion: if a finite group
G
acts on a finite CW complex
Y
such that
for every nontrivial subgroup
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}G
and every connected component
C
of
the fixed set
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Y^{H}
, the Euler characteristic
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}(C)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, then the
orbifold extension group
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}((\mathrm{EG}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}Y)/G)
lies in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{FH}(Q)
.

That is, there exists a finite CW complex with the given fundamental group
whose universal cover is rationally acyclic.

17.4.2
3b. Concrete Instantiation via Reflection Lattice

The E2 obligation is discharged by the following construction (details in
the candidate-construction note
3
3

3

https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem7r-s2b-candidate-construction.md
):

1.

Arithmetic lattice with reflection.
Take an arithmetic uniform lattice

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}\mathrm{Isom}(H^{n})
containing a reflection
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}
, as provided by
Douba-Vargas Pallete (arXiv:2506.23994, Remark 5). Choose
n

even

(e.g.,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
or
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6}
).

2.

Congruence cover.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(I)
be a sufficiently deep
principal congruence subgroup. Then
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}\ H^{n}
is a closed hyperbolic
manifold, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}
induces an involution
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{bar}
on
M
.

3.

Extension.
Set
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}G\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{bar}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Z/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
acting on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}}:\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}M
.
The orbifold extension gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}Z/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
, where

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
is a cocompact lattice (finite extension of cocompact
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
) with
order-2 torsion.

4.

Fixed-set Euler check.
The fixed set
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{Fix}}}\nolimits({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}_{bar})
is a (possibly
disconnected) closed, embedded, totally geodesic hypersurface
(arXiv:2506.23994). Each component has dimension
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
. Since
n
is even,

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
is odd, and every closed odd-dimensional manifold has Euler
characteristic zero. So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}(C)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for every fixed component
C
.

5.

Fowler application.
The only nontrivial subgroup of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Z/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
is itself.
All fixed components have zero Euler characteristic. By Fowler's Main
Theorem,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{FH}(Q)
.

E2 status: discharged
for this lattice family.

17.5
4. Obligation S: From Finite Complex to Closed Manifold

Obligation S is treated as discharged in this branch via the rotation route
(S-rot-II); this section records the previously open obstacles and how
S-rot-II resolves them.

Problem 7 asks for a
closed manifold

M
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(M)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}(M_{tilde};Q)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
. Obligation E2 gives a finite CW
complex with these properties; the upgrade to a closed manifold is the
remaining open problem.

17.5.1
Available geometric data

The construction in Section 3b provides:

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
: a torsion-free cocompact lattice in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{Isom}(H^{n})
, with
n
even

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6}
.

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H^{n}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
: a closed hyperbolic
n
-manifold.
M
is a closed manifold
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(M)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
and contractible universal cover.

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}
: an involution on
M
with fixed set
F
— a closed, totally
geodesic
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
-manifold.

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}\rtimes Z/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
: the target group (cocompact lattice with
order-2 torsion).

•

Y
: a finite CW complex with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(Y)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
and
Y~
rationally
acyclic (from Fowler's theorem).

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{n}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
: a compact orbifold with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{orb}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
, mirror
singularity along the image of
F
.

The torsion-free quotient
M
already solves the problem for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
. The
difficulty is entirely in passing from
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
— from the
torsion-free lattice to its Z/2-extension.

17.5.2
Approach I: Wall surgery via the FH(Q) complex

Idea.
Use
Y
(the FH(Q) complex) as the target of a surgery problem.
Promote
Y
to a rational Poincare complex, find a degree-1 normal map to
it, and show the surgery obstruction vanishes.

Obstacles.

1.

Poincare complex structure.
FH(Q) gives a finite CW complex
Y
with
rationally acyclic universal cover, but not a Poincare complex. Since

Y~
is rationally contractible (by rational Hurewicz from rational
acyclicity + simple connectivity), the Serre spectral sequence collapses
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}(Y;Q)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H_{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma};Q)
, which satisfies rational PD
(Section 2). But promoting homology-level PD to a chain-level Poincare
complex structure on
Y
has not been done.

2.

Degree-1 normal map.
Even with Poincare complex structure, a degree-1
normal map
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}f:M_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}Y
requires the Spivak normal fibration of
Y
to
admit a topological reduction. No construction of this reduction has been
given.

3.

Surgery obstruction.
The obstruction
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\sigma}(f){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}L_{n}(Z\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573})\text{tensor}Q

is not known to vanish. The Farrell-Jones conjecture (which holds for

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
) identifies this L-group with equivariant L-homology, but the
resulting computation has not been completed. See the obstruction-analysis note
4
4

4

https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem7r-s3b-obstruction.md

for the FJ reduction framework and a conjectural (but unverified)
localization of the obstruction.

Status.
This approach has three successive obstacles, each open.
See the setup note
5
5

5

https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem7r-s3a-setup.md
for the detailed analysis.

Dead Path — Approach II: Equivariant Surgery (Reflection Route)

Idea.
Work directly with the closed manifold
M
and the involution

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}
. Eliminate the fixed set
F
by equivariant surgery to obtain
M'

with a free Z/2-action. Then
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M^{\prime}/(Z/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
is a closed manifold with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
.

Blocking obstruction.
The equivariant surgery framework of
Costenoble-Waner (arXiv:1705.10909) requires a
codimension-2 gap
hypothesis
: fixed sets of distinct isotropy subgroups must differ in
dimension by at least 2 (Condition 3.4(3)). For our Z/2-action on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M^{n}

with codimension-1 fixed set
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F^{n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
, this gap condition
fails
.
The Dovermann-Schultz framework (Springer LNM 1443) similarly requires
gap conditions.

Status.
Blocked for the reflection construction (codimension-1 fixed
sets). However, the approach becomes viable for
rotational involutions

(codimension-2 fixed sets) — see Approach IV below.

Dead Path — Approach III: Orbifold Resolution

Idea.
The quotient
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{n}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
is a compact orbifold with mirror
singularity. Resolve the singularity to produce a closed manifold with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
and rationally acyclic universal cover.

Obstacles.

1.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
preservation.
Standard orbifold resolution (e.g., cutting along
the mirror and doubling) typically changes the fundamental group.
A resolution preserving
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
would need to be specifically
constructed.

2.

Rational acyclicity.
The resolution must preserve (or establish)
rational acyclicity of the universal cover.

Status.
Not explored beyond this sketch.

Process Note — Dimension-Parity Tension

The E2 obligation (for reflections) requires
n

even
(so the fixed set
has odd dimension and Euler characteristic zero). But the surgery obstruction
computation (Approach I) and the AHSS structure of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{n}(Z\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573})\text{tensor}Q

have better vanishing properties when
n
is
odd
. This tension between
E2 and S is a central difficulty for the reflection construction.

Why reflections cannot work in odd dimension.
For a reflection on

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
, the fixed set is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k}
— a closed hyperbolic manifold of
even dimension. By the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, such manifolds have
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.
So Fowler's criterion fails. This is not an artifact of the construction; it
is forced by Riemannian geometry.

17.5.3
Approach IV: Rotation route (resolves the parity tension)

Idea.
Replace the reflection (codimension-1 involution) with a

rotational involution
(codimension-2 fixed set) in
odd
ambient
dimension. An order-2 isometry of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
that fixes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}

(codimension 2) is a “rotation by
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}^{\prime}
' in a normal 2-plane.

Why this resolves the tension.
For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
odd:

•

Fixed set
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
has dimension
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
(odd), so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
. Fowler
criterion is satisfied:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{FH}(Q)
.

•

Surgery obstruction lives in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(Z\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573})\text{tensor}Q
, which has
favorable parity (odd total degree forces odd
p
in AHSS terms).

•

The codimension-2 gap hypothesis (required by equivariant surgery theories,
Costenoble-Waner arXiv:1705.10909) is
satisfied
, so Approach II
(equivariant surgery) becomes available as a method for the S-branch.

Lattice construction: DISCHARGED.
Details in the rotation-lattice
construction note.
6
6

6

https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem7r-rotation-lattice-construction.md
Summary:

1.

Quadratic form.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}k\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Q(\sqrt{(}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})),O_{k}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Z\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\sqrt{(}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}.
Define

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}f\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}\sqrt{(}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}))x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}...{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}x_{n}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
variables.
Under the two real embeddings of
k
,
f
has signatures
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(n,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(n{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
. So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{SO}(f)
gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{SO}(n,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
at one place and a compact
group at the other.

2.

Uniform lattice.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathrm{SO}(f,O_{k})
is a cocompact arithmetic
lattice in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{SO}(n,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
(Borel-Harish-Chandra; cocompactness by Godement
criterion since
f
is anisotropic over
k
).

3.

Order-2 rotation.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\sigma}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\operatorname{diag}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},...,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
is in

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{SO}(f,O_{k})
: it preserves
f
(negates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
in the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

summand), has determinant
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
, and has integer entries. Its fixed set on

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{n}
is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
(codimension 2).

4.

Congruence subgroup.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}(I)
for ideal
I
coprime to 2:
torsion-free (Minkowski),
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\sigma}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H^{n}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
is a closed
hyperbolic manifold. The extension
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}Z/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\sigma}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}
is a cocompact lattice with order-2 torsion.

5.

Fowler application.
Fixed set has dimension
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}k-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
(odd), so

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
. By Fowler's Main Theorem:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{FH}(Q)
.

E2 status for rotation route: DISCHARGED.

Obligation S resolution.
The manifold upgrade proceeds
via two sub-options available on the rotation route:

•

S-rot-I (Wall surgery in odd dimension).
Same three-obstacle structure
as Approach I (Poincare complex, normal map, obstruction), but the
obstruction computation benefits from odd L-theory parity.

•

S-rot-II (Equivariant surgery on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(M,{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\sigma})
).
The codimension-2 gap
hypothesis is satisfied, so the Costenoble-Waner framework (arXiv:1705.10909)
applies. The equivariant surgery obstruction is computable in principle.

This is the most promising remaining path for Problem 7.
The lattice
existence bottleneck is resolved. The open problem reduces to computing a
surgery obstruction (either Wall or equivariant) in the structurally
favorable odd-dimensional setting.

17.6
5. Remark: Absence of Smith-Theory Obstruction

A natural objection is that the order-2 element in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
would force fixed
points on any rationally acyclic covering space via Smith theory. This does

not
apply here: Smith theory over
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Z/p
constrains mod-p homology of
fixed sets, but the construction targets
Q
-acyclicity. Over
Q
, the
transfer homomorphism shows that fixed sets can be rationally trivial without
contradicting Smith's theorem.

This section addresses a natural objection and explains why it does not
apply. It does not contribute to the constructive argument, which is entirely
in Sections 3-4.

17.7
6. Theorem

Theorem.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
be a cocompact lattice extension

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}Z/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
constructed from an arithmetic lattice in

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{Isom}(H^{n})
containing an order-2 isometry, via either:

•

(Reflection route)
Section 3b: reflection lattice in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{Isom}(H^{n})
,

n
even,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}6}
.

•

(Rotation route)
Approach IV: rotation lattice in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{Isom}(H^{n})
,

n
odd,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}7}
. See the rotation-lattice construction note.

Then:

(a)
(Unconditional)

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathrm{FH}(Q)
: there exists a finite CW complex

Y
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(Y)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

17.8
7. Path to Full Closure

Resolving obligation S requires computing a surgery obstruction. The
rotation route (Approach IV) has discharged the lattice-existence question
and is now the primary path. See the hypothetical-wirings note

for
full wiring diagrams.

Active path: Approach IV (rotation route).
The lattice construction
is complete (see the rotation-lattice construction note
10
10

10

https://github.com/tothedarktowercame/futon6/blob/master/data/first-proof/problem7r-rotation-lattice-construction.md
). E2 is
discharged. Two sub-options for obligation S:

1.

S-rot-II (Equivariant surgery — OBSTRUCTION VANISHES).

The Costenoble-Waner codimension-2 gap hypothesis is satisfied. The
cut and cap
method (Browder, López de Medrano) applies.

Key result: the equivariant surgery obstruction
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\theta}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(integrally).

The argument has two layers:

•

Rational vanishing (Step A: flat-normal-bundle argument).
The
normal bundle
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu}
of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}M
is flat (totally geodesic embedding). By
Chern-Weil, e(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}Q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.
This forces the intersection form on S(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu}
)
to be rationally hyperbolic
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\theta}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}Q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}.

•

Integral vanishing (trivial holonomy).
For the congruence ideal
I with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Norm(I)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
(e.g., I
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
(3)): the integrality constraint on
rotation matrices over
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{Z}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
, combined with the congruence condition
g
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\equiv
I mod I, forces the holonomy representation
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\rho}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathrm{SO}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
to be
trivial. So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu}
is a trivial bundle, e(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
in H
2
(F; Z), and the
circle bundle S(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}
S
1
is a product. The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\int}
intersection
form on H
3
(F
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}
S
1
; Z) is block off-diagonal (hyperbolic), giving

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\theta}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}
L
8
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{Z}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
).

With
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\theta}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
:
The equivariant “cut and cap” surgery succeeds. Remove
the tubular neighborhood N(F) from M, obtaining
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}W\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}M
\ int(N(F)) with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\partial
W
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
S(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\nu})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}
S
1
and free Z/2-action on W. Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\theta}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, a cap V
exists with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\partial
V
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}F{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}
S
1
and free Z/2-action. Set M'
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
W
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\cup}
V. Then
N
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
M'/(Z/2) is a closed manifold with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}
1
(N)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Gamma}
and rationally
acyclic universal cover.

See the S-rotation obstruction analysis

2.

S-rot-I (Wall surgery in odd dimension).
Fallback. Same three-obstacle
structure as Approach I but with favorable odd L-theory parity and
strictly fewer AHSS terms than the reflection case.

Dead Path — Deprioritized Approaches

Deprioritized paths:

3.

Approach I (Wall surgery, reflection route).
Three successive open
obstacles with structural headwinds (even L-theory parity).

4.

Approach III (orbifold resolution).
No technique identified.

5.

Approach II (equivariant surgery, reflection route).
Blocked by
codimension-2 gap hypothesis (Costenoble-Waner, arXiv:1705.10909).
