Chapter 18
Problem 8: Lagrangian Smoothing of Polyhedral Surfaces with 4-Valent Vertices

18.1
Problem Statement

A
polyhedral Lagrangian surface

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
is a finite polyhedral complex all
of whose faces are Lagrangian 2-planes, which is a topological submanifold of

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
. A
Lagrangian smoothing
of K is a Hamiltonian isotopy
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{t}
of smooth
Lagrangian submanifolds for t in (0,1], extending to a topological isotopy on

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
, with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}K
.

Question:
If K has exactly 4 faces meeting at every vertex, does K
necessarily have a Lagrangian smoothing?

18.2
Answer

Yes.
A polyhedral Lagrangian surface with 4 faces per vertex (with
distinct adjacent faces) always admits a Lagrangian smoothing. The vertex
spanning property (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},\ldots,e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
spans
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
) is automatic — it follows from
the Lagrangian and distinct-face conditions alone (Section 3), with no
additional hypotheses needed.

Process Note — Confidence

Confidence: Medium-high.
The v2 argument (symplectic direct sum
decomposition at each vertex) is verified numerically: 998/998 random valid
4-valent configurations give Maslov index exactly 0. The decomposition proof
is algebraic (not just heuristic). Vertex smoothing uses the product structure
(corners in symplectic factors); edge smoothing uses generating functions.
The Hamiltonian isotopy property is established via the Weinstein neighborhood
theorem (edges) and the vanishing flux in simply-connected
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
(vertices).

18.3
Solution

18.3.1
1. Setup: Lagrangian planes in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}

Identify
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathbb{C}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
with symplectic form
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathrm{d}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\wedge\mathrm{d}y_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}\mathrm{d}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\wedge\mathrm{d}y_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}.A
Lagrangian plane is a 2-dimensional subspace L where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}|_{L}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
. The space
of Lagrangian planes (the Lagrangian Grassmannian) is:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}U({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})/O({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

This is a manifold of dimension 3 with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}))\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Z
(the Maslov class).

Each face of K lies in a Lagrangian plane. At each edge, two Lagrangian faces
meet at a dihedral angle. At each vertex, 4 Lagrangian faces meet.

18.3.2
2. Local structure at a 4-valent vertex

At a vertex v, the 4 faces
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
(in cyclic order around v) are
Lagrangian half-planes meeting along edges:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}12}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\cap L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}23}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\cap L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}34}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\cap L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}41}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}\cap L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

These 4 edges are rays from v. Each face is spanned by its two boundary edges:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{i-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},i},e_{i,i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})

The Lagrangian condition
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}|_{L_{i}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
requires:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(e_{i-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},i},e_{i,i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\text{for each}i(\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{mod}}}\nolimits{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})

18.3.3
3. The symplectic direct sum decomposition (key new argument)

Theorem (v2).
The 4 edge vectors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}12}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}23}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}34}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}41}}
satisfy
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(e_{i},e_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
for all i (mod 4). This forces
a symplectic direct sum decomposition:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})
are symplectic 2-planes
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}|_{V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.

Proof.
Write the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
matrix in the basis (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}).
The
conditions
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(e_{i},e_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
kill 4 of the 6 independent entries,
leaving only:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},b\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

(Nonzero because
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
is non-degenerate and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
is a
basis — see the nondegeneracy hypothesis below.)

Lemma (vertex spanning).
At every vertex of a polyhedral Lagrangian
surface with 4 faces per vertex (with distinct adjacent faces sharing
1-dimensional edges), the 4 edge vectors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
span
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
.

Proof.
Suppose for contradiction that the 4 edge vectors lie in a
3-dimensional subspace H
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\subset\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}.
Then all 4 faces
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
span(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{i-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},i},e_{i,i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})\subset H.

Step 1.
The restricted form
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}|_{H}
has rank 2: since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
is
non-degenerate on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
, its restriction to a codimension-1 subspace has a
1-dimensional kernel ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{ker}}}\nolimits({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}|_{H})\subset H.

Step 2.
Every Lagrangian 2-plane L
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\subset
H contains ell. Proof: L is
2-dimensional with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}|_{L}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
. If L were transverse to ell (i.e.,
L
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cap
ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}

{
0
}
), then L would project isomorphically onto a 2-plane in
H/ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cong

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
. But
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}|_{H}
descends to a non-degenerate 2-form on H/ell
(since ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{ker}}}\nolimits({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}|_{H})),
and a 2-plane in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
on which a
non-degenerate 2-form vanishes must be
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
. Contradiction. So ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\subset
L.

Step 3 (key).
Each edge
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{i}
is the intersection of two adjacent faces:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cap

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cap

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
, etc. Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
are
distinct 2-planes (distinct faces), their intersection is exactly
1-dimensional:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{dim}}}\nolimits(L_{i}\cap L_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
, so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cap

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{i})
.

By Step 2, ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\subset L_{i}
and ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\subset L_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},
so ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\subset L_{i}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cap

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{i})
. Since both ell and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{i})
are 1-dimensional subspaces and
one contains the other, ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{i}).

Applying this to all four edges:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
ell
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}).
That is, all four edge vectors are proportional.

But then
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
is 1-dimensional, contradicting
the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
is a 2-plane.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\square

(Note: this argument is purely algebraic — it uses only that adjacent faces
are distinct Lagrangian 2-planes sharing a 1-dimensional edge. No
topological submanifold condition is needed.)

In the reordered basis (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}),
the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
matrix is:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}-a,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},b\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},-b,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

This is block diagonal:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},a){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}(V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},b)
, a symplectic direct sum.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\square

Consequence:
Each Lagrangian face decomposes as a direct sum of lines:

\displaystyle L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})\subset V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

\displaystyle L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})\subset V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

\displaystyle L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})\subset V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

\displaystyle L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})\subset V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

Each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i}
takes one line from
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
and one from
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
, which is automatically
Lagrangian in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
restricted to a line is zero).

18.3.4
4. Maslov index vanishes exactly

The Maslov index of the vertex loop
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}
(2) decomposes via the direct sum:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}_{j}
is the winding number of the component loop in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{j}
.

In
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
:
The loop traces

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})

This is a back-and-forth path (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
), not a
winding loop. The winding number is 0.

In
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})
:
Similarly:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})

Winding number 0.

Total Maslov index:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.

Numerical verification:
998/998 random valid 4-valent configurations
(with edge-sharing enforced) give Maslov index exactly 0. In contrast,
random quadruples without edge-sharing give nonzero Maslov index ~45% of
the time (see scripts/verify-p8-maslov-v2.py).

18.3.5
5. Why 4 is special: the 3-face obstruction

For a 3-face vertex, the 3 edge vectors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
must satisfy:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

This means
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
vanishes on ALL pairs — the span is an isotropic subspace.
But in (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}),
the maximum isotropic dimension is 2 (= half the
dimension). Three independent isotropic vectors cannot exist.

Therefore: a non-degenerate 3-face Lagrangian vertex is impossible in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
.

The 4-face condition is precisely the right valence: it gives enough edges to
span
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
while the cyclic
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
-orthogonality creates the symplectic direct
sum that forces Maslov index 0.

We do not claim a general obstruction for valence
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\geq
5 here; the proof
establishes sufficiency for valence 4.

18.3.6
5a. Vertex smoothing via the product structure

The key observation: the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
decomposition (Section 3) gives K near
each vertex v a product structure, enabling a direct smoothing that bypasses
both crease smoothing and Lagrangian surgery.

Product decomposition of K near v.
Choose Darboux coordinates adapted to

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
: let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
have coordinates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(x,y)
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
dx
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\wedge
dy, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

have coordinates
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(u,v)
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
du
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\wedge
dv (so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
).
Orient the edge vectors so that:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}),e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}),e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}),e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})

(Here
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},
consistent with the decomposition
in Section 3. This can always be arranged by a linear symplectomorphism.)

The 4 faces in these coordinates are:

\displaystyle L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{y\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},u\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}},\text{sector}x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},v\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

\displaystyle L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{y\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},v\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}},\text{sector}x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},u\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

\displaystyle L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},v\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}},\text{sector}y\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},u\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

\displaystyle L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{span}}}\nolimits(e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},u\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}},\text{sector}y\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},v\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

Define the “corner” curves in each factor:

\displaystyle C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{(x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}):x\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\cup}{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},y):y\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}\subset V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(\text{positive}x\text{and}y\text{axes})

\displaystyle C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{(u,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}):u\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\cup}{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},v):v\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}\subset V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(\text{positive}u\text{and}v\text{axes})

Claim:
K
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cap
B(v, r)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}C_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
(product of the two corners) for small r.

Verification:
A point
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(p,q)
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
has
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
or

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},y)
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(u,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
or
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},v)
, giving 4 cases — exactly the 4 faces

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},...,L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
listed above.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\square

Smoothing.
Replace each corner
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{j}
with a smooth curve
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{j}^{sm}\subset V_{j}

that agrees with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{j}
outside a ball of radius
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
around the origin:

\displaystyle C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}:\text{smooth curve}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{(x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})}\text{for}x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},y)}\text{for}y\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},

\displaystyle\text{smooth through the origin}(e.g.,\text{the curve}({\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\cos}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\theta},{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\sin}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\theta})

\displaystyle\text{reparameterized to match the axes outside the transition})

\displaystyle C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}:\text{analogous}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

Explicitly: parameterize
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}
as
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(t)
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R
, where

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(t)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(t,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t\geq{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(t)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},
-t) for t
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},

and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
is a smooth embedded curve for t
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}.
(Such a
curve exists: it is a smooth rounding of the right angle at the origin.)
Define
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(t)
analogously for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}
.

Lagrangian property of the product:
The smoothed surface

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K^{sm}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}
is a product of smooth curves in the
symplectic factors. A curve in a 2-dimensional symplectic manifold is
always Lagrangian (its dimension is 1
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
half of 2, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
restricted
to a 1-submanifold is zero for dimensional reasons). The product of
Lagrangian submanifolds in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}(V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
is Lagrangian
in (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
): for tangent vectors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(u_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},u_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(w_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},w_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}
, we have

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}((u_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},u_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}),(w_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},w_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}))\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(u_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},w_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(u_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},w_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},w_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
are tangent to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}
(1-dimensional in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
) and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},w_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}

are tangent to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}
(1-dimensional in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
).
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\square

Smoothness:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K^{sm}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}
is the image of the smooth
map
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(t_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},t_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(t_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}),{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}(t_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})),
which is a smooth immersion
(the tangent vectors d
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/\mathrm{d}t_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
and d
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}/\mathrm{d}t_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
are nonzero and lie
in complementary subspaces
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
). So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K^{sm}
is a smooth Lagrangian
surface, including at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0})
.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\square

Agreement:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K^{sm}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}K
outside the region where either
factor was modified (|p|
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
or
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|q|{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
), so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K^{sm}

agrees with K outside a neighborhood of v of radius
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})
.

18.3.7
6. Edge smoothing (crease smoothing along edges between vertices)

After resolving all vertices (Section 5a), the remaining singularities of K
are edge creases: compact arcs connecting the boundaries of resolved vertex
neighborhoods. Along each edge arc, two adjacent Lagrangian faces meet at a
dihedral angle. These creases are smoothed by the standard generating-function
interpolation:

Lemma (edge crease smoothing).
Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},L_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
be two Lagrangian half-planes
meeting along a compact edge arc e (a segment of a common boundary ray, away
from any vertex). Then there exists a smooth Lagrangian surface agreeing with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
on one side and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
on the other.

Proof.
Choose Darboux coordinates (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},y_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},y_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})
with e along the

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
-axis. Each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}L_{i}
is locally the graph y
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
grad
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{i}(x)
for quadratic
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{i}
.
Define
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}}(x)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}(x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon})S_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(x){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}(x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
/eps))
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(x)
for a
smooth cutoff
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}
. The graph y
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
grad
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}}
is Lagrangian (graph of the
exact 1-form
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{d}S_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}}
). For any fixed
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, this gives a smooth Lagrangian
surface replacing the crease along e.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\square

(The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{C}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
control issues noted in earlier drafts do not arise here: the
crease smoothing is applied only along edge arcs that are INTERIOR to edges
(between vertex neighborhoods), not at vertices. Since the edge arcs are at
positive distance from all vertices, and the smoothing width eps can be
chosen small relative to this distance, the edge smoothings are localized
in thin tubular neighborhoods of the edge arcs.)

18.3.8
7. Global smoothing

Step 7a: Resolve vertices.
For each 4-valent vertex
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v_{i}
, choose a ball

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{i}
of radius
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}r_{i}
centered at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v_{i}
, where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}r_{i}
is small enough that:

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{i}
contains no other vertex
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v_{j}(j{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}i),
and

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{i}
intersects only the edges and faces incident to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v_{i}
.

Such radii exist because the vertex set is finite and discrete in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
.
Within each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{i}
, apply the product smoothing (Section 5a) using the

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
decomposition from Section 3. This replaces K
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cap

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{i}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

with the smooth Lagrangian surface
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}
.

Commutativity:
Since the balls
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\{}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\}}
are pairwise disjoint, the
vertex smoothings have disjoint support and commute.

Step 7b: Smooth edges.
After all vertex resolutions, the remaining
singularities are edge creases — compact arcs connecting the boundaries
of resolved vertex neighborhoods. Each edge arc lies along the intersection
of two Lagrangian faces and is a compact 1-manifold (with boundary on
the spheres
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\partial
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{i}
). These creases are resolved by the generating-function
interpolation of Section 6.

The edge smoothings have support in tubular neighborhoods of the edge arcs.
These neighborhoods can be chosen to be disjoint from each other (since the
edges are disjoint away from vertices, which have already been resolved) and
from the vertex balls (since the edge arcs start at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\partial B_{i}
, outside
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{i}
).
Therefore the edge smoothings commute with each other and with the vertex
smoothings.

Compatibility at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\partial B_{i}
.
The vertex smoothing (Section 5a) agrees with
the original polyhedral K outside a neighborhood of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}v_{i}
of radius
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}r_{i}
.
So on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\partial B_{i}
, the surface is still polyhedral (two flat faces meeting at an
edge). The edge crease smoothing (Section 6) begins at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\partial B_{i}
, where the
surface is already flat. Since both the vertex smoothing (product of smooth
curves) and the edge smoothing (graph of exact 1-form) produce Lagrangian
surfaces, and they agree on the overlap (both equal the original flat faces
on the annular region
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}|x|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}r_{i})
, they glue to a globally smooth
Lagrangian surface.

Step 7c: Global Hamiltonian isotopy.
Each smoothing is parameterized by
a width parameter t (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
for vertices, eps for edges). As
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
, the
smoothing region shrinks and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}K\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}K_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}C^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}},
giving the topological
isotopy on [0, 1]. It remains to show
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{t}
is a
Hamiltonian
isotopy
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.

Edge smoothings are Hamiltonian.
The family of generating functions

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{t}(x)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}(x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}/t)S_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(x){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}(x_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}
/t))
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(x)
defines a smooth
1-parameter family of Lagrangian graphs y
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
grad
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}S_{t}(x){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}T^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
. By
the Weinstein Lagrangian neighborhood theorem (Weinstein 1971, Theorem 6.1),
a smooth family of exact Lagrangian submanifolds in an exact symplectic
manifold (here
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T^{{\color[rgb]{0.15,1,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}}}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}y
dx) is generated by a Hamiltonian:
the 1-form
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\iota}_{d/\mathrm{d}t}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega})|_{K_{t}}
is exact (it equals
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}d(S_{t}|_{K_{t}})
),
so the isotopy is Hamiltonian with generating function
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathrm{d}S_{t}/\mathrm{d}t

evaluated on the Lagrangian.

Vertex smoothings are Hamiltonian.
The vertex smoothing is a product:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{t}^{\text{vertex}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}(t){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}(t){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
. Each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{j}^{sm}(t)

is a smooth 1-parameter family of curves in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(V_{j},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{j})

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\cong
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
, dx
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\wedge
dy).
For the contractible arc family used here, the induced closed 1-forms are
exact, so this local isotopy is Hamiltonian. This is
because
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{j}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
is simply connected, so the flux homomorphism
Flux:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\pi}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(\mathrm{Symp}){\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}H^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
(L; R) is trivial (no non-Hamiltonian symplectic
isotopies exist). Concretely: the velocity field d/dt of the isotopy

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{j}^{sm}(t)
is a symplectic vector field along
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{j}^{sm}(t)
; contracting
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}_{j}
gives a closed 1-form on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{j}^{sm}(t)
, which is exact because

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}(C_{j}^{sm}(t);
R)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
0 (each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}C_{j}^{sm}(t)
is a noncompact embedded arc
in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
— the smoothed corner — hence contractible). The primitive
is the Hamiltonian
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{j}(t)
. The product isotopy on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}V_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
is Hamiltonian
with generating function
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}(t){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}H_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}(t)
(see McDuff-Salamon, 3rd ed.,
Exercise 3.18: product of Hamiltonian isotopies is Hamiltonian).

Composition.
The vertex smoothings (in disjoint balls
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B_{i}
) and edge
smoothings (in disjoint tubular neighborhoods) have pairwise disjoint
compact support. Each is a compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy. Their
composition is a compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy (McDuff-Salamon,

Introduction to Symplectic Topology
, 3rd ed., Proposition 3.17: the
group
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrm{Ham}_{c}
(M,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
) of compactly supported Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms
is a group under composition).

18.3.9
8. Topological constraints

For K to be a compact topological submanifold with 4 faces per vertex,
Euler's formula constrains the topology. For quadrilateral faces:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}-}E{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}F\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}
(K), with 4F
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
2E and 4V
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
2E, giving
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.
So K is topologically a torus or Klein bottle.

For non-quadrilateral faces with 4 per vertex, other topologies are possible
but
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(higher genus surfaces).

18.3.10
9. Summary

The smoothing exists because:

1.

The 4-face + Lagrangian condition forces a symplectic direct sum
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}

at each vertex (vertex spanning is proved algebraically; opposite edges span
complementary symplectic 2-planes)

2.

This decomposition forces Maslov index exactly 0 (algebraic, not just generic)

3.

Near each vertex,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
(product of corners). Smoothing each corner
gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K^{sm}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}^{sm}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}C_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}^{sm}
, a smooth Lagrangian surface (product of
curves in symplectic factors). No Lagrangian surgery needed at vertices.

4.

Edge creases (between vertices) are smoothed by generating function interpolation

5.

All smoothings are Hamiltonian isotopies, composable to give global
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{t}

6.

The 3-face case is impossible (isotropic dimension bound), explaining
why 4 is the right valence

Revision — Corrections from v1

•

v1 claimed
Maslov index vanishes by
alternating orientations relative to J.

Replaced
with the precise symplectic direct sum argument: the
edge-sharing constraint forces
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
to be block diagonal in the
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},e_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}})
basis, giving
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
algebraically.

•

v1 stated
the 3-face obstruction as “nonzero Maslov index.”

Corrected:
3-face Lagrangian vertices don't have nonzero Maslov index —
they're impossible (3 isotropic vectors can't span 3D in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
).

•

numerical verification.
Added
via verify-p8-maslov-v2.py:
998/998 valid configurations give
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
; comparison with non-edge-sharing
quadruples (55% have
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\mu}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
) confirms the constraint is essential.
