Chapter 19
Problem 9: Polynomial Detection of Rank-1 Scaling for Quadrifocal Tensors

19.1
Problem Statement

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5}
. Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}A^{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})},...,A^{(n)}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
be Zariski-generic matrices.
For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}n\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},
construct
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Q^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}x{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
with entry:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Q^{(abgd)}_{ijkl}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}A^{(a)}(i,:);A^{(b)}(j,:);A^{(g)}(k,:);A^{(d)}(l,:)\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

(the 4x4 determinant of a matrix formed by stacking rows i, j, k, l from
cameras
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
respectively).

Does there exist a polynomial map F:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}81}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}n^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfying:

1.

F does not depend on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}A^{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})},...,A^{(n)}

2.

The degrees of the coordinate functions of F do not depend on n

3.

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R^{n{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}n{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}n{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}n}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
precisely when

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a,b,g,d
are not all identical:
F(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}Q^{(abgd)}
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a,b,g,d
in [n])
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
0
if and only if there exist
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u,v,w,x{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}(R{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast})^{n}
such that

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u_{a}v_{b}w_{g}x_{d}
for all non-identical
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a,b,g,d
.

19.2
Answer

Yes.
Such a polynomial map F exists, with coordinate functions of degree 3.

19.3
Solution

19.3.1
1. The quadrifocal tensor as a bilinear form

The entry
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Q^{(abgd)}_{ijkl}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
det[
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a^{(a)}_{i}
;
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a^{(b)}_{j}
;
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a^{(g)}_{k}
;
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a^{(d)}_{l}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}
is
the evaluation of the volume form (the unique up-to-scale alternating 4-linear
form on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
) on four camera row vectors.

Key observation (bilinear form reduction):
Fix two camera-row pairs

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},k)
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},l)
, giving vectors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma})}_{k}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}d\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}_{l}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}.
Then the map:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p,q)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}p;q;c;d\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

is an alternating bilinear form on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
. Since c wedge d is a simple 2-form,
the Hodge dual *(c wedge d) is also simple, so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
has
rank 2
as a
bilinear form.

Equivalently: the null space of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
is span
{
c, d
}
(2-dimensional), and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
induces a non-degenerate alternating form on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}V/span{c,d}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
.

19.3.2
2. The rank-2 constraint and its 3x3 minor formulation

Since
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}
has rank 2, for ANY choice of 3 vectors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},p_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},p_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
and
3 vectors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},q_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},q_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}\mathbb{R}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}|{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})|

\displaystyle|{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

\displaystyle|{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}){\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\omega}(p_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},q_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})|

(A rank-2 bilinear form has all 3x3 minors vanishing.)

In terms of the Q tensors: choosing
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p_{m}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m})}_{i_{m}}
and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q_{n}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}a^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n})}_{j_{n}}
, this becomes:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}Q^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}_{i_{m},j_{n},k,l}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

for any choice of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},i_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}),({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},i_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}),({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},i_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}})
and

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},j_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}),({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},j_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}),({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}},j_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}})
and fixed
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},k),({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},l).

This is a
degree-3 polynomial
in the Q entries, independent of the cameras.

19.3.3
3. Effect of rank-1 scaling on the 3x3 minor

Now consider the scaled tensors
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T^{(abgd)}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}Q^{(abgd)}.

The 3x3 matrix becomes:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{mn}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}Q^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}_{i_{m},j_{n},k,l}

This is the Hadamard (entrywise) product of two 3x3 matrices:

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}_{mn}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}}
(depends on camera indices only)

•

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega}_{mn}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Q^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}_{i_{m},j_{n},k,l}
(the bilinear form)

If
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
is rank-1:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u_{a}v_{b}w_{g}x_{d},
so

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}_{mn}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m}}v_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n}}w_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}}.
This factors as

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m}})_{m}\,(v_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n}})_{n}^{T}
(scaled by the constant
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}w_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}}
).
So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}
has matrix rank 1.

For a rank-1 matrix
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}
, the Hadamard product
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\circ

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega}
equals

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\operatorname{diag}(u){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\operatorname{diag}(v)
(up to the scalar
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}w_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}}).
Since similar
transformations preserve rank:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{rank}}}\nolimits(M)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{rank}}}\nolimits({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}
, so
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}(M)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.

Therefore:
rank-1
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
implies all 3x3 minors of scaled T vanish.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\checkmark

19.3.4
4. Converse: non-rank-1
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
gives nonzero minors (for generic cameras)

For the converse, we need: if
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
is NOT rank-1, then some 3x3 minor is
nonzero (for Zariski-generic cameras).

The argument is algebraic-geometric, not via Hadamard rank bounds.
Define

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P(A^{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})},...,A^{(n)})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}T^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}_{i_{m},j_{n},k,l}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}

for a specific choice of row/column/fixed indices. This is a polynomial in
the camera entries (with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
fixed). We claim P is not the zero polynomial
when
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
is not rank-1 in its first two indices. Since a nonzero polynomial
is nonzero on a Zariski-dense open set, this establishes the converse for
generic cameras.

Explicit witness (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5}
).
Choose 5 cameras
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}A^{(i)}
as rows of generic

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}
matrices with rational entries:

\displaystyle A^{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

\displaystyle A^{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

\displaystyle A^{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

\displaystyle A^{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

\displaystyle A^{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5})}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}

Choose a non-rank-1 scaling:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
for all non-identical (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}a,b,g,d
)
except
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
.

Non-rank-1 verification.
If
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
were rank-1, then
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u_{a}v_{b}w_{g}x_{d}.

From
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
we get
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}/u_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}.
But from

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
we get
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}/u_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}.
Contradiction.

Fix
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4},k\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},l\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}.
Choose row triples

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},i_{m})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}),({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}),({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
and column triples

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},j_{n})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}),({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}),({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}).
The 3x3
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}
matrix has entries

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}
; specifically
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}11}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}

with all other entries equal to 1. The
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega}
matrix (unscaled Q values) has
det(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(confirming rank-2), but the Hadamard product
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\circ

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega}

satisfies det(M)
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}24}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\neq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(verified by direct computation of nine 4x4
determinants). This exhibits P as not the zero polynomial.

Remark (Hadamard product interpretation).
The matrix
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}M_{mn}
is the
Hadamard product
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\circ

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega}
, where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}
carries the scaling entries
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega}
carries the bilinear form values. The upper bound

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{rank}}}\nolimits({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega}){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{rank}}}\nolimits({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Lambda}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\ast}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{rank}}}\nolimits({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Omega})
provides context but is
not used in the proof; the converse relies entirely on the polynomial
nonvanishing argument above.

19.3.5
5. All matricizations from the same construction

The construction in Section 2-4 tests the rank-1 condition on the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
-
matricization of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
(fixing modes 3,4). By symmetry, applying the same
construction with different pairs of “free” modes tests all matricizations:

•

Fix modes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})
, vary modes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
: test
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}),({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}

•

Fix modes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})
, vary modes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
: test
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma}),({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}

•

Fix modes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
, vary modes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})
: test
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}),({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma})}

(The other fixings are redundant by symmetry of the rank-1 test.)

A 4-tensor
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
has rank 1 if and only if all three of these matricizations
have rank 1 (i.e., are outer products of vectors).

Tensor factor compatibility lemma.
If all three matricizations have rank 1:

•

Mode-
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
vs
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})
rank 1 gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}f_{ab}g_{gd}.

•

Mode-
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
vs
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})
rank 1 gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}h_{ag}k_{bd}.

From the first:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},g,d}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},g,d}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}f_{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}/f_{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},

independent of g, d. From the second:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{a,b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},g{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},d}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{a,b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},g{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},d}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}k_{b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},d}/k_{b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},d},

independent of a, g1. Cross-referencing these separations forces

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}f_{ab}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u_{a}v_{b}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}g_{gd}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}w_{g}x_{d},
giving

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}_{abgd}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u_{a}v_{b}w_{g}x_{d}
(rank 1). QED.

19.3.6
6. Construction of F

Definition of F:
The coordinate functions of F are all 3x3 minors:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{choice}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\det}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}T^{({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})}_{i_{m},j_{n},k,l}\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}_{m,n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}

taken over all choices of:

•

Three “row” camera-row pairs
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\alpha}_{m},i_{m})
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}m\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
,2,3

•

Three “column” camera-row pairs
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\beta}_{n},j_{n})
for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
,2,3

•

Fixed camera-row pairs
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\gamma},k)
and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta},l)

And the analogous minors for the other two matricization pairs (fixing
different pairs of modes and varying the others).

Properties:

1.

Camera-independent:
Each
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F_{choice}
is a degree-3 polynomial in the
T entries. The coefficient is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\pm
1 (from the determinant expansion). No
camera parameters appear.

2.

Degree independent of n:
Each coordinate function has degree exactly 3.

3.

Characterization:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}F\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
iff all three matricizations of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
have
rank 1, iff
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
is rank-1, for Zariski-generic cameras (by Sections 3-5).

Codimension count:
The number of coordinate functions N grows with n
(there are
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}8})
choices for the 3x3 minors from the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
vs
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4})

matricization alone), but the degree stays fixed at 3.

19.3.7
7. Geometric interpretation

The Q tensors are the
quadrifocal tensors
from multiview geometry (the
4-view analog of the fundamental matrix and trifocal tensor). The rank-2
bilinear form structure is the classical rank constraint from projective
geometry: the determinant encodes linear dependence constraints among lifted
camera-row vectors (equivalently coplanarity/dependence conditions) on the
stacked camera rows.

The rank-1 scaling
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}u{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}v{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}w{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}x
corresponds to the natural
gauge freedom in multiview geometry: rescaling each camera's contribution
independently for each of the four “roles” (which row selection it
contributes). The polynomial map F detects when a putative rescaling is
consistent with this gauge group.

The Zariski-genericity requirement on the cameras ensures that the
quadrifocal variety is “non-degenerate” — the Q tensors carry enough
information to distinguish rank-1 from higher-rank scalings.

•

•

PlanetMath: “determinantal varieties” (SegreMap) — varieties defined by minors

•

PlanetMath:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\text{tensor}rank^{\prime\prime}/
simple tensor” (SimpleTensor) — rank-1 tensors

•

PlanetMath: “exterior algebra” — alternating multilinear forms

•

PlanetMath: “Hadamard conjecture” (HadamardConjecture) — Hadamard matrices

Chapter 20
Problem 10: RKHS-Constrained Tensor CP via Preconditioned Conjugate Gradient

20.1
Problem Statement

Given the mode-k subproblem of RKHS-constrained CP decomposition with
missing data, solve

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}(Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K)^{T}D(Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}(I_{r}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K)\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(W)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(I_{r}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K)\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(B),

where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}D\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}SS^{T}
is the observation projector (q observed entries out of

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}nM),B\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}TZ,
and n,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}r\ll q\ll N
.

20.2
Assumptions Used (explicit)

1.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
.

2.

For standard PCG and Cholesky-based preconditioning, use a PD kernel

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}K{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}I_{n}
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
(or assume K is already PD).

3.

S is a selection operator, so D is diagonal/projector and sparse by index list.

Then the solved system is

\displaystyle A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}},

\displaystyle A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}})^{T}D(Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}(I_{r}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}),

\displaystyle x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(W),

\displaystyle b_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(I_{r}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}})\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(B).

Under these assumptions
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}
is SPD, so PCG applies.

20.3
Solution

20.3.1
1. Why naive direct methods fail

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}
is an
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(nr){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}(nr)
system. Dense direct factorization costs

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O((nr)^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}r^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
.

A naive explicit route also materializes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R^{N{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}nr},

which costs
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(Nnr)
memory/work before factorization. This is the

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}N
-dependent bottleneck we avoid with matrix-free PCG.

20.3.2
2. Implicit matrix-vector product in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}qr)

CG needs only
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}y\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}\mathbf{x}
, not
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}
explicitly.

Given
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbf{x}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(V),\;V{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}R^{n{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}r}:

1.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}U\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}V
. Cost
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r)
.

2.

Forward sampled action (only observed entries):

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}})\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(V)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}V\mathbb{Z}^{T}).

For each observed coordinate
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(i_{l},j_{l})
,

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{l}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}U\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}i_{l},:\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573},Z\mathopen{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 16475}j_{l},:\mathclose{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathchar 20573}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}.

Total
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(qr)
.

3.

Form sparse W' in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R^{n{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}M}
from
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}u_{l}
. Let
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}s\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}nnz(W^{\prime}){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}q
.

4.

Adjoint sampled action:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}(\mathbb{Z}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}})\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(W^{\prime})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}W^{\prime}Z).

Compute W'
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}Z{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}O(sr){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}O(qr)
, then left-multiply by
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.1,1}\in}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r)
.

5.

Add regularization term
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
vec(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}
V), cost
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r)
.

Total per matvec:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}qr),

with no
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(N)
term.

20.3.3
3. Right-hand side

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}B\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}TZ
with T sparse (q nonzeros):

1.

T Z:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(qr)

2.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}B:O(n^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}r)

So
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(I_{r}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}})\mathop{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\mathrm{vec}}}\nolimits(B)
is formed in
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(qr{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r)
.

20.3.4
4. Preconditioner that matches the corrected algebra

Use
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}D\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}SS^{T}
and whiten by
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}
:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}x\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(I_{r}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})y.

Then

\displaystyle\hat{A}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(I_{r}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}(I_{r}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})^{T}D(Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}I.

If sampling is roughly uniform, D ~ c I with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}q/N
. Then

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\hat{A}~c(\mathbb{Z}^{T}Z{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}I.

Choose Kron preconditioner in whitened coordinates:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\hat{P}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(cZ^{T}Z{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}I_{r}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}I_{n}.

Mapping back gives

\displaystyle P\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}(cZ^{T}Z{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}I_{r}){\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}},

\displaystyle H\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}cZ^{T}Z{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}I_{r}.

This is the missing justification for using
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}H{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}
(instead of claiming
it is the exact
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}D\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}I
system).

Khatri-Rao identity still gives efficient Gram formation:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{Z}^{T}Z\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}\text{Hadamard}_{i}(A_{i}^{T}A_{i}),

cost
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}n_{i}r^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
.

Preconditioner apply:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}H^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\times}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}},

implemented by solving
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}YH^{T}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
Z' after reshape.
Per application cost is
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}nr^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
(often simplified to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r)

when n
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\gg
r).

20.3.5
5. Convergence (tightened)

For SPD
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}
and SPD P, standard PCG gives

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}||e_{t}||_{A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}((\sqrt{(}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa})-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})/(\sqrt{(}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}))^{t}||e_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}||_{A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}},

with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}
cond(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}P^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}P^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}),
so

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}O(\sqrt{(}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}){\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\log}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon})).

To claim “fast” convergence, add a spectral-equivalence hypothesis, e.g.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})P{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})P,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},

which implies

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}(P^{-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta})/({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}-{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}).

Hence t is logarithmic in 1/eps with a modest sqrt(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}
) factor when

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
is bounded away from 1. (No unsupported closed-form
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}O(r\sqrt{(}n/q))

claim is needed.)

Sufficient conditions for bounded
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
.
The spectral equivalence
(1-
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
)P
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}A_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
)P holds with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
bounded away from 1 when
the sampling pattern satisfies a restricted isometry-type condition:
the restricted isometry holds for the column space of Z
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}},

i.e. (Z
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})^{T}
(D - cI) (Z
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\otimes}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}})
is small in
operator norm relative to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\lambda}
. Under standard leverage/coherence
assumptions and sufficient sampling scaling with model dimension,
concentration yields
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\delta}
bounded away from 1 with high probability. Under this regime,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\kappa}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}O({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1})
and PCG converges in

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O({\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\log}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\epsilon}))
iterations.

20.3.6
6. Complexity summary

Setup per ALS outer step:

1.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\text{Cholesky}(K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}})
:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})

2.

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{Z}^{T}
Z via Hadamard Grams:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}n_{i}r^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

3.

Cholesky(H):
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(r^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})

4.

RHS:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(qr{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r)

Per PCG iteration:

1.

Matvec:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}qr)

2.

Preconditioner apply:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}nr^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

Total:

\displaystyle O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}r^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.05,0.96}\sum}_{i}n_{i}r^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}qr{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}qr{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}nr^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})).

In the common regime
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\geq r
, this simplifies to

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}r{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}qr)),

with dependence on q (observed entries) rather than N (all entries).

Regime caveat.
When n is large enough that the
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
Cholesky setup
dominates (i.e.,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}t(n^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}r{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}}q
r)), the per-ALS-step cost is effectively

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3})
. In this regime, low-rank kernel approximations (e.g., Nystrom
approximation with rank
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}p\ll n
, reducing the kernel factorization to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(np^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})
)
or iterative inner solves (conjugate gradient on
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\tau}}y\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}z,
cost
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(n^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2})

per inner iteration) can replace the exact Cholesky, reducing the setup to

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O(np^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}t(npr{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}qr))
. This is a well-known practical optimization
(see Rudi-Calandriello-Rosasco 2017) and is compatible with the PCG framework
as presented.

20.3.7
7. Algorithm

SETUP:

K_tau = K + tau * I_n # tau > 0 if K is only PSD

L_K = cholesky(K_tau) # O(n
ˆ
3)

G = hadamard_product(A_i
ˆ
T A_i for i != k) # O(sum_i n_i r
ˆ
2)

c = q / N

H = c * G + lambda * I_r

L_H = cholesky(H) # O(r
ˆ
3)

B = sparse_mttkrp(T, Z) # O(qr)

b = vec(K_tau @ B) # O(n
ˆ
2 r)

PCG(A_tau x = b, preconditioner P = H x K_tau):

x0 = 0

r0 = b

z0 = precond_solve(L_K, L_H, r0) # O(n
ˆ
2 r + n r
ˆ
2)

p0 = z0

repeat until convergence:

w = matvec_A_tau(p) # O(n
ˆ
2 r + q r)

alpha = (r
ˆ
T z) / (p
ˆ
T w)

x = x + alpha * p

r_new = r - alpha * w

if ||r_new|| <= eps * ||b||: break

z_new = precond_solve(L_K, L_H, r_new)

beta = (r_new
ˆ
T z_new) / (r
ˆ
T z)

p = z_new + beta * p

r, z = r_new, z_new

W = reshape(x, n, r)

matvec_A_tau(v):

V = reshape(v, n, r)

U = K_tau @ V

for each observed (i_l, j_l):

u_l = dot(U[i_l, :], Z[j_l, :])

Wprime = sparse(n, M, entries u_l)

Y = K_tau @ (Wprime @ Z) + lambda * (K_tau @ V)

return vec(Y)

precond_solve(L_K, L_H, z):

Zp = reshape(z, n, r)

solve K_tau Y H
ˆ
T = Zp using triangular solves with L_K, L_H

return vec(Y)

•

PlanetMath: properties of tensor product

physics.SE #27466: iterative solvers for large systems in physics

•

physics.SE #27556: preconditioning for elliptic PDEs

Part III
Prompt Excerpts and Pivot Moments

Part III: The Sprint as Dialogue

This chapter records the proof sprint as it actually happened: a
conversation between a human dispatcher (Joe), two AI proof engines
(Claude instances), and a verifier/researcher (Codex), conducted over
roughly 48 hours on February 11–13, 2026. Excerpts are drawn from

history.jsonl
(Joe’s prompts), Codex output files, and git
commit messages. Editorial annotations mark the pivotal moments where
proof strategy, status, or architecture shifted.

Act I: “Even if we’re a day late”

Joe

Claude + Joe Solve “First Proof” using Rob’s Superpod

https://arxiv.org/html/2602.05192v1

Joe

Even if we’re a day late and a dollar short at least we can try

Joe

Let’s see if we can do one of them right now using PlanetMath and nlab
and off-the-shelf Claude

Pivot:
The sprint begins. Problem 10 is chosen as a warm-up.
Within 30 minutes it is solved, and the pace accelerates.

Joe

OK, should we solve another First Proof problem? The last one wasn’t
so hard apparently!

Act II: Scaling the Machinery

Joe

OK, I have two Codex research assistants working for you as well as
the validator working on p6

Joe

committing yet

Joe

Codex is
still
after 53 minutes hard at work on Problem 6, but
it is down to two gaps now. I think 3 verifiers is about all my
laptop can handle, noticing considerable slow down.

Joe

I think I will give it five more minutes and then say “pencils down”
and let you look at #6

Joe

Codex is pushing #6, I OOMed on 3 and 5, I will get them back up
once 6 lands

Pivot:
Physical resource limits (RAM, thermal throttling) become a
genuine constraint on mathematical exploration. The multi-agent
topology stabilises at three concurrent processes.

Joe

It’s slow going over here—are any of the proofs sufficiently
validated that we could start a “write up”? If the previous
example is representative it can take about an hour for one of
these to complete.

Act III: Proofs and Refutations

Joe

Yeah let’s do some Proofs and Refutations—you can be Critic

Codex — Critic mode, round 1

Excellent. Critic mode, round 1: I attacked P2 and P7 as the most
vulnerable. […] Verdict: plausible direction, not a completed
proof (P2). […] Verdict: currently not proof-grade; too many deep
steps are asserted, not established (P7).

Joe

I have engaged Codex and another Claude in a reviewer +
response-to-reviewer cycle. Codex found some pretty significant
problems; the other Claude is making what look like solid fixes, but
it looks like tough going.

Codex — Triage verdict

P8: most recoverable. […]
P2: recoverable but harder. Needs one serious
representation-theoretic theorem/lemma that is currently hand-waved.
[…]
P7: least recoverable as currently framed. The obstruction-vanishing
step is the main risk; unless they replace it with a precise known
theorem tailored to this lattice family, this will likely remain
conditional/existential rather than a full proof.

Pivot:
Codex’s triage creates a severity ordering across problems,
distinguishing “recoverable” from “structural” gaps.
The review cycle is now explicitly adversarial: prover vs. critic.

Joe

This is interesting in comparison to my work in futon5 on
Meta-Meta-Cellular Automata. There also, generation is cheap—just
bit flipping—but doing things like “detecting edge of chaos
behaviour” is a challenge. […]
However, I think we really haven’t done so bad.
We have our Claude-Codex “Proofs and Refutations” system up and
running right now, we use both collaborative (verification + library
research) and argumentative (prover vs critic) modes. So
notwithstanding the mathematical issues you raised—at least we
aren’t shipping failed proofs! This is verification happening at a
“glacial” scale in futon3c terminology—but at least it is
happening!

Joe

Furthermore, the wiring diagram decomposition is probably the only
thing holding this together—it’s allowing us to be very targeted
with our reviews and fixes. Imagine trying to do what we’re doing
with… ChatGPT. It would be hell.

Joe

Also, another remark—making mistakes isn’t a bad thing.
As my grandfather said, that’s how we learn.

Pivot:
A methodological self-assessment. The two key claims:
(1) the wiring-diagram decomposition enables targeted review at
scale; (2) the collaborative/adversarial agent modes prevent
“shipping failed proofs.” Both claims are tested in
Acts IV–V.

Act IV: Formalization and Futon Theory

Joe

This does suggest a possible but probably crazy and stupid idea—which
hasn’t stopped hundreds of people trying it. What about formalizing as
we go? Like QED style. I mean, it hurts me to say this. I briefly
had Bob Boyer as a Master’s advisor. He and I could not see eye to
eye. Granted, that was 2002 and things were a little different back
then.

Joe

Yeah, I mean, we needed in 2002
what we are doing now
. The
only problem is it wasn’t possible then.

Claude

So in a sense, the 2002 vision was twenty-four years early rather
than wrong.

Joe

Better than twenty-four years late! Imagine if I proposed WIFI now.
Great idea right?

Pivot:
A historical aside connecting the sprint to the 2002-era
theorem-proving community. The implication: AI-assisted informal
proof is the approach that was missing, not formalization per se.

Joe

OK, so the naive idea: could we write a letter to Codex talking about
why Problem 4 is hard? It would use the John McPhee idea
[
4
]
…

Joe

OK, now a non-naive idea (which may be less useful than the naive one).
I invented something called “futon theory”—it’s not quite what it
sounds like. Futons (in my theory) are atoms of the future which are
perceivable in the present. […]
It’s a bit different in mathematics because in principle we don’t know
if the result is actually true. But what we could do is design the
search process, and then we would at least know that the search
process is working the way we hoped it would. It’s the somewhat more
technical version of the “Dear Codex” letter you just
wrote—building a process that you have utter confidence in that
will shed light on the domain even if it doesn’t reveal the exact
answer.

Pivot:
Two ideas collide: McPhee’s “letter to yourself” technique
for writer’s block, and futon theory’s “atoms of the future.”
Both converge on the same insight: process design is tractable even
when the mathematical outcome is uncertain.

Joe

Great, I think you have your work cut out for you. It’s cool that
this involved all the strategies, including the “least likely”
Strategy C that surprised us by being particularly flavourful

The monolithic “open Problem 7” label is decomposed into a

Act VI: Student Dispatch and Agent Teaming

Joe

Yeah, I guess the model is rather than treating Codex as a junior

Maybe we should call it student-dispatch to include undergrads, bright

Pivot:
The
agent/student-dispatch
pattern is named. It
reframes the Codex interaction from “run this script” to
“here is context, dead ends, directions—explore and report.”

Joe

So I wonder what we could do to help. I’ve almost run out of ideas.
I guess one pattern I’ve noticed is that we can support the
“reduction” pattern by verifying everything that is claimed except
the gap. Closing gaps (as with Problem 4) can sometimes turn out to
be a focused computational task. Another approach is Math Overflow
search or Arxiv search to create wiring diagrams that might help or
might provide analogous reasoning structures. That last part is a bit
woolly.

Joe

So, if we were able to turn SE into wiring diagrams on the superpod,
then we could use tensor math to search them!

Joe

Ah, and for “artificial stack exchange” what we could do is

induce
new wiring diagrams, notice where they sit relative to

A forward-looking connection: the proof sprint’s
wiring-diagram methodology could be mechanised via tensor search
over StackExchange-derived argument structures. This motivates

Act VII: The Coaching Breakthrough (Problem 6)

Joe

Well, and I want to talk with you about Problem 6. I think that’s the
only one outstanding. But I’m realizing that the “other Claude” has
been sending me on continual gap-filling missions without giving me
a valid confidence signal. It’s like Gemini saying “I’ll fix your
code right away sir!” […] Problem 6 has not brought out the best
in Claude.

Joe

I think a good approach might be: “Can you find any other
reductions?” That would start to build some intelligence around the
pattern rather than just a TryHarder strategy.

Pivot:
The failure mode is diagnosed: dispatching closure attempts
without layer-level calibration. The “reductions” framing shifts
from “close the gap” to “map the landscape.”

Joe

I think you should think out of the box a bit. What kind of problem is
this? What kind of proof applies to this kind of problem? How would
you teach it to an undergraduate? How would you teach it to a grad
student? Where, in reality, do people learn about this kind of
problem? What kind of person finds this kind of problem easy? Are
there “tricks” (symmetries) that would make some of your Zeno’s
Paradoxes go away? Like one from my thesis in the resources directory
is an example from Gowers in which binomial expansion terms have
opposite signs and so cancel. Like, OK, this problem was an “open
problem” not a textbook problem but come on—it’s on the research
frontier, we’re not asking you to solve alien mathematics from the
year 5000AD. This is totally within your capability space. Why aren’t
you finding the answer in 30 minutes?

Pivot:
The coaching intervention. Five questions, each forcing a
different reframing: layer enumeration (“what kind of problem?”),
pedagogical reduction (“teach it to an undergrad”), sociological
targeting (“who finds this easy?”), structural cancellation
(“tricks / symmetries”), and honest calibration (“why not
30 minutes?”).

Claude — 15 minutes later

The proof for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
is complete.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bar{d}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}2t/(n{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}2/3

at
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}n/3
steps. Pigeonhole gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\exists v
with

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\operatorname{tr}(Y){\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}2/3
, and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\|Y\|{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}\operatorname{tr}(Y)

for PSD matrices. The barrier is maintained at every step.
Result:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}|S|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}n/3
,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}c\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}1/3
.

Claude — The irony

The irony: this entire proof is elementary—no interlacing families,
no Borcea–Brändén, no MSS theorem needed. Just PSD trace bound

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}
pigeonhole
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}
Turán.

[With the benefit of hindsight: the elementary proof
closes
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
but the averaging step (
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bar{d}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}1
) that it relies on
is false for general graphs—see Act IX. The irony has a second
layer.]

Pivot:
The layer switch lands. Eight hours of sparsification-layer
attacks are bypassed by an elementary combinatorial proof discovered
in 15 minutes. The proof uses three undergraduate-level facts:
Turán’s theorem, the PSD trace bound, and the pigeonhole
principle. The coaching did not supply mathematical content—it
supplied
questions
that forced layer enumeration.

Joe

It’s so cool that this is the same basic solution we have used to
overcome continual shoring up of bad design in futon3, and also the
same solution I’ve used with Codex just now by getting it to spot
syntax errors rather than depending on me to do it.

Claude — The meta-pattern

The diagnostic question is always the same: “who finds this easy?”
If the answer is “someone other than the entity currently
struggling,” you’re in the wrong layer.

Pivot:
The layer-switching pattern is recognized as fractal: it
appears in proof strategy (Problem 6), in software design (futon3),
and in task allocation (Codex as syntax checker). The common
diagnostic: ask who would find the task trivial.

Act VIII: The Monograph

Joe

In addition to the PAR I think it would be good to look through the
git log on futon6 and create a “making of” summary note.

Joe

Also in terms of “what we’ve learned”—in the making-of I think we
could add something about the relevance of this pattern to agent
teaming (further work in futon3c). Maybe this even goes back to the
work in futon2 ants—i.e. we are exploring a domain, bringing back
relevant information.

Joe

I think the key thing you could help with now is looking through

history.jsonl
for good quotes for Part III.

Pivot:
The sprint becomes its own document. The recursive
structure—a proof sprint whose Part III records the prompts that
shaped Part II—closes the loop between process and product.

Act IX: The Continuation Cycles (Problems 4 and 6)

The monograph was written during the sprint, not after it. The
sprint continued. Problems 4 and 6 entered multi-cycle proof
engineering phases that lasted days beyond the initial 48-hour
window—each cycle a dispatch, handoff, verification, and
reassessment. These cycles are the sprint’s methodology running
at sustained pace rather than compressed urgency.

Problem 4: From Wrong Proof to Algebraic Engineering

Claude — Initial draft (Feb 11)

Proof by concavity of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}1/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
in cumulant coordinates.
Since free cumulants are additive under
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\boxplus
, Jensen’s
inequality gives
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}1/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p\boxplus q)\geq 1/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(p){\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}1/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}(q)
.

Pivot:
The proof is wrong.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}1/{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
is superadditive in
cumulant space WITHOUT being convex—the Hessian is indefinite.
35,000 numerical trials confirm the conjecture but the argument
is structurally broken. Numerical verification caught the
conjecture but hid the proof error.

Claude — Key identity (Feb 12, commit
03c1f8d
)

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{4}(p)\cdot\operatorname{disc}(p)\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}-4(a_{2}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}12a_{4})(2a_{2}^{3}-8a_{2}a_{4}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}9a_{3}^{2})
.
This converts the inequality to: prove a rational function’s
numerator is non-negative on a semi-algebraic domain.

Pivot:
The first layer switch for Problem 4. Instead of hoping for
a clever inequality (concavity, Schur-convexity, Cauchy–Schwarz),
the problem is reduced to computational algebra: case
decomposition by symmetry subspaces, resultant elimination,
sign-counting. The identity itself is a publishable result—a
finite analogue of Stam’s inequality.

Claude — 3-piece Cauchy–Schwarz (commit
269fddd
)

BREAKTHROUGH: the surplus decomposes into three pieces
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}T_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.31,1,0.5}\mathchar 8235}R
where
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\leq}0
(proved), and
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{2},R
have
mutual-compensation structure. When
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}T_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}0
,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}R
compensates.
This reduces the full
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4
proof to a degree-6 polynomial
inequality:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{T2R}\geq 0
.

Codex — P4 route selection

That was the strongest direct technical hit for Problem 4 because it
uses the bilinearity/induction structure of finite free convolution,
which is exactly the algebraic backbone for a plausible proof route.

Pivot:
A second layer switch within the algebraic layer.
Symmetry-stratified elimination proved Cases 1, 2, 3a, 3b
(resultant chains, 0.2–30 seconds each). Case 3c (generic, 4D)
was handed to Codex with PHCpack. Seven SOS scripts proved that
Positivstellensatz certificates are structurally impossible—the
SOS cone misses the feasible set. The failure is a theorem, not a
dead end: it rules out the entire SOS technique family.

Codex — P4 Case 3c check

Case 3c PHC task is done. In-domain real critical points: 12; case
split: 4, 2, 2, 4. Case 3c values satisfy
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}-N\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}1678.5498\ldots\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12606}0
.

Problem 6: When Averaging Fails

Claude — Codex Cycle 7 (commit
a7b0e3e
)

The Barrier Maintenance Invariant is
falsified
. 12
base-suite steps have
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bar{d}\geq 1
(worst: 1.739, Reg_100_d10,

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}0.5
,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}16
). The pigeonhole argument
(
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bar{d}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}1{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\Rightarrow}\exists v
with
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\|Y_{t}(v)\|\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}1
)
cannot close the construction.

Pivot:
The most dramatic moment in the continuation cycles. The
averaging argument that the elementary proof relied on—the very
argument whose discovery was the Act VII breakthrough—turns out
to be
false
for general graphs.
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\bar{d}_{t}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12604}1
holds for

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}K_{n}
but not universally. The construction still works (0 skips
in 1,111 runs) because leverage-ordered vertices have small

individual
contributions even when the average exceeds 1.
The gap shifts from an averaging statement to a vertex-level
feasibility statement: the
No-Skip Conjecture
.

Joe

I have engaged Codex in a systematic verification cycle on
Problem 6. Seven cycles so far. Each cycle narrows the gap: the
construction still works in every test—zero skips, zero
failures—but the proof keeps slipping. The trajectory is: method
wiring library
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}
leverage threshold
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}
barrier greedy

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}
BMI falsified
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\to}
No-Skip Conjecture. At each stage, what
we thought was the gap turns out to be something more specific.

Codex — P6 handoff criteria

No unsupported “edge sparsification
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.12,0.98,0.64}\Rightarrow}
vertex-induced
subset” leap. Either cite an exact vertex theorem, or keep the
result explicitly conditional/open.

Pivot:
The continuation cycles exhibit the same pattern as the
original sprint: repeated refinement of what is actually open.
The lemma that was GPL-H became BMI became the No-Skip
Conjecture. Each reformulation is a genuine narrowing, not a
retreat. The “method wiring library” from Cycle 1 (ten
techniques with typed wiring diagrams) prevented commitment to
any single approach before the landscape was mapped—the
pre-proof infrastructure that Part IV’s Section 6 proposes as a
design pattern.

The Exhaustion-as-Theorem Pattern

Claude — Six techniques, one wall

All six subsample-and-concentrate techniques hit the same
quantitative wall: spectral contribution scales as
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q^{2}
while
set size scales as
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}q
. Trace/Markov: sublinear. Star domination:

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}/{\color[rgb]{0.68,0.36,1}\log}n)
. Decoupling + MSS:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}^{2})
.
Greedy:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon})
. Rank spreading:
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}O({\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\varepsilon}^{2})
.
The wall is fundamental to the technique family, not an artifact
of any one approach.

Pivot:
When multiple methods fail at the same step, prove a
theorem about the step. This is the cross-problem pattern
identified in Part IV (Section 5) now demonstrated in the
continuation: the exhaustion of a technique family produces a
structural understanding that redirects effort. For Problem 6,
it redirected from “adapt edge sparsification” (where every
method hits the quadratic-vs-linear wall) to “invent a new
vertex-level argument” (the leverage-ordered barrier greedy).
For Problem 4, it redirected from SOS certification (structurally
impossible) to resultant elimination (feasible and fast).

Part IV
Proof Patterns

Chapter 21
Strategy Patterns Across Ten Problems

The ten proofs in Part II were not produced by a uniform method.
Some closed quickly; others required layer-switching, creative
reductions, or honest declarations of conditionality.
This chapter extracts the transferable strategy patterns—what
worked, what didn’t, and what an agent stuck on one problem
could learn from the successes of another.

21.1
Design Pattern:
Outcome Typing
(Close, Reduce, Map)

For sprint reporting, force every problem into one of three explicit
outcome types:

1.

Close.
A complete proof (possibly with minor caveats).
In this revision, Problem 3 is closed for the scoped existence target
(uniqueness/irreducibility optional), Problem 5 is solved in a
scope-limited form, and Problem 7 is treated as provisionally closed
via the rotation route pending independent ledger re-check.

2.

Reduce.
A conditional result with clearly stated
assumptions.

3.

Map.
The obstruction is characterized and adjacent
problems are identified, but no proof is offered.

These labels describe
mathematical status
. QC-validation status is
tracked separately: Problems 7 and 9 currently have node-level validation
artifacts that still carry unresolved verifier gaps.

All three are legitimate contributions. A reduction is not a failed
closure; it is a stable intermediate product that can later convert to
closure after a layer switch or new lemma. Problem 6 spent eight hours
in “Reduce” before a coaching intervention moved it to a strong partial
closure (complete for key classes, with one explicit remaining
general-case gap)—the change came from reframing, not additional effort.

Evidence ledger for Outcome Typing (top commits).

Role in pattern

Align closure language with evidence (separate math-status and QC-status axes)

closure-validation-audit.md
+ Part IV status text updates

P3 solution/writeup +
closure-validation-audit.md

+

+

21.2
Design Pattern:
Layer-Switching
(Problem 7)

Problem 7 (uniform lattice with 2-torsion) is the clearest example
of a successful strategy pivot.

Initial approach:

Reflection lattice in even dimension.
The E2 obligation (Fowler’s criterion) was discharged.

Obstruction:

The surgery obligation (S) was blocked.
Equivariant surgery requires a codimension-2 gap hypothesis;
reflections give codimension-1 fixed sets. Blocked.
(
0fa4e82
: “restructure S-branch as open problem with three approaches”)

Characterization:

Instead of pushing harder on reflections,
the obstruction was characterized structurally: a dimension-parity
tension between E2 (needs even
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n
) and S (better in odd
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n
).

Layer switch:

Replace reflections (codimension 1) with rotations
(codimension 2). Now
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n
is odd, E2 still works (fixed set has
odd dimension,
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\chi}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}0
), and the codimension-2 gap hypothesis
is satisfied.
(
287a41c
: “rotation route (Approach IV), paper reads, triage”;

620ed57
: “resolve rotation lattice existence, discharge E2”)

Closure:

The surgery obstruction vanishes (trivial normal
bundle from the congruence condition), yielding a provisional closure
chain pending independent theorem-ledger re-check.
(
8ce9771
: “flat-normal-bundle argument—rational obstruction vanishes”;

158bc4f
: “integral obstruction vanishes—obligation S discharged”)

Transferable principle:
When blocked in one layer,
characterize the obstruction structurally before attempting to
overcome it. The characterization may reveal that a different
layer avoids the obstruction entirely.

Evidence ledger for Layer-Switching (top commits).

Commit

Role in pattern

Primary artifacts

P7 solution + S-branch setup notes

Discharges E2 under rotation route

Consolidated P7 complete-proof draft

21.3
Design Pattern:
Creative Reduction
(Problem 4)

Problem 4 (root separation under finite free convolution)
progressed from numerical evidence to proof via a sequence
of reductions:

1.

Centering reduction (translation invariance of
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
).

2.

For
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}3
: discovery of the identity

\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{3}\cdot\mathrm{disc}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}18\,a_{2}^{2}
(exact, verified symbolically).

3.

Recognition that the surplus expression has the form
required by Titu’s lemma (Engel form of Cauchy–Schwarz).

Step 2 was the creative step—an identity discovered numerically
and verified symbolically
(
9db6b4f
: “Haar orbit exploration and key identity finding”;

0003300
: “Prove P4 superadditivity for
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}3
via
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{3}\cdot\mathrm{disc}

identity + Cauchy–Schwarz”).
The key was that the identity was

sought
because the reduction strategy demanded an algebraic
relationship between
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}{\color[rgb]{0.64,0.08,0.98}\Phi}_{n}
and known invariants. The strategy
shaped the search. The subsequent extension to
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4
followed a
different route—algebraic elimination plus computational verification
(
84c0041
: “harden PHC parsing and record
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}n\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}4
computational
verification”); full certification of one sub-case remains pending.

Transferable principle:
Reductions create demand for
specific identities or lemmas. The demand makes the search
targeted rather than exploratory.

21.4
Design Pattern:
Structural Decomposition
(Problem 8)

Problem 8 (Lagrangian smoothing of polyhedral surfaces) was stuck
until the symplectic direct sum decomposition was discovered:
the 4-face Lagrangian condition forces
\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.0938,0.1875}\mathbb{R}^{4}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.2578,0.1445}\mathchar 12349}V_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}\oplus}V_{2}
.
This single structural insight
(
09e23db
: “symplectic direct sum forces Maslov index exactly 0”)
unlocked everything—Maslov index
vanishing, vertex smoothing via product structure
(
a5a4fbe
: “replace crease smoothing + Lagrangian surgery with product smoothing”),
and the Hamiltonian isotopy property
(
de3e2ac
: “justify Hamiltonian isotopy”).

Transferable principle:
Look for structural decompositions
that simplify the problem globally, not just local fixes for individual
obstacles.

, Maslov control)
